SOURCE CITATION CONCORDANCE
The Thiele Machine — Devon Thiele

Source-reference contexts for the book's results. Labels and statement titles follow
the current book; the surrounding prose keeps earlier wording in places and should be
compared with the current book and source files.
This is lookup material, excluded from the rendered book.

[1] Let me be straight with you
\emph{The universal certification result.} The universal certification result is this: when the
yes-state set is finite and the reading is permanent, every move that raises the reading merges
states. Consequently, every cost schedule satisfying merge pricing charges that move at least one
mark, and every run from no to yes costs at least one. This is a theorem over all systems
satisfying those premises. It isn't a property peculiar to the implementation in Part IV
(\code{nec\_f\_perm\_flip\_merges\_yes\_finite} and \code{nec\_f\_toll\_from\_merges\_yes\_finite},
NecFMerge.v). The counting under it is classical, the finite cousin of Zermelo's recurrence
argument and folklore among automata theorists; what's mine is reading it as a price on a ledger,
and Appendix~\ref{sec:whats-mine} gives the neighbours. Three layers stay separate through the book. The first is the structural fact that a
permanent raise merges states, which needs no prices at all. The second is the pricing premise,
merge pricing, which turns that fact into a ledger bound. The third is the small machine of Part IV,
which enforces earned certification on top of both.

[2] Let me be straight with you
\begin{itemize}
\item Linear logic (Girard 1987) treats an assumption as a resource that gets used up. It
constrains what a program, or a proof, may consume. The toll constrains the model's step
function instead. Same instinct, different layer.
\item Foundational proof-carrying code (Appel 2001) checks safety proofs against the smallest
trusted base it can: a logic plus the machine's semantics. Here no proof rides along with
the program. The step rule writes the receipts as the run goes.
\item Session types (Honda 1993) constrain how programs talk to each other so the conversation
can't go off script. The toll is about what one computation has paid to establish.
Different problem.
\item No Free Lunch (Wolpert and Macready 1997): averaged over every problem, whatever an
optimiser gains on some problems it gives back on others. That's an average over a whole
problem space, and the edge is paid for elsewhere. The toll charges one step in one run,
with no refund anywhere.
\item Kolmogorov complexity (Kolmogorov 1965): the structure in an object is the length of the
shortest program that prints it. That's a property of a finished string, with no steps and
no moment of paying. The toll is a law about a step.
\item Cook and Levin (Cook 1971; Levin 1973): checking can be cheap while finding looks
expensive, and whether finding is really harder is the open P-versus-NP question, a gap in
how difficulty grows with size. The toll puts a fixed floor under the one step that
commits, however easy or hard the finding was.
\item The thermodynamic cost of choosing (Rovelli 2024): settling a binary choice at temperature
T gives off at least kT ln 2. That's the closest thing to the toll itself I've found, because it
charges a commitment. An object or an average isn't what it prices. But it's physics, indexed to a
temperature, and its choice is unpredictable, a branching, and stable only for a while, where
the toll's commit is a deterministic merge that stays. The toll charges the commitment in a model of computation, with no
temperature in it. Closest neighbour. A different chair.
\item Persistent copies (Ouldridge and ten Wolde 2017; Ouldridge, Govern and ten Wolde 2017):
making a copy that stays correlated with its original after the two come apart has a
thermodynamic bill. The nearest physics I've found to ``learning free, committing priced.'' It's
heat for a correlation, in chemistry; the toll is a count in a model of computation.
\item Thermodynamics of finite automata (Ouldridge and Wolpert 2023): if the smallest automaton
for a language has a letter that sends two states to one, recognising that language on a
device that repeats one local step produces entropy, and if the smallest automaton only
reshuffles, recognition can be free. That's the chairs with a physical price on them, and one
of their worked examples is the latch's language turned inside out. It's the closest neighbour
of all to the road the toll is derived on; Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary} says exactly
what's left over for me.
\item CerCo, the Certified Complexity project of Amadio and coauthors, builds a verified C compiler that carries the object
code's execution cost back up to annotations on the source. There the cost decorates the
program and survives compilation. Here it lives in the model's step rule.
\item Reference monitors and execution monitors (Anderson 1972; Schneider 2000; Azevedo de
Amorim and coauthors 2015). A small piece every action passes through, enforcing a rule on
every program. Schneider argued that a monitor watching a run can enforce only rules whose
breaking shows up at a finite point, and ``no certificate without an earlier passing check and
commit'' is one of those; the micro-policies check a tag rule at every instruction, proved in
Coq. That's the move the small machine's step makes, so this one is the house itself. What's added is the edition-bound check-then-commit with a paid, permanent record on top,
and the derivation of why the payment can't be zero. Kevin Hamlen, Greg Morrisett and Fred
Schneider (2006) went on to sort which rules monitors, program rewriters and static checkers can
each enforce, and they differ, which is one reason this book doesn't claim that every enforcement
mechanism is a Thiele machine underneath.
\item Trusted components in replicated services (Chun and coauthors 2007; Levin and coauthors
2009; Veronese and coauthors 2013; Decouchant and coauthors 2022; Clement, Junqueira, Kate and
Rodrigues 2012): a small record inside each replica that a replica taken over still can't make
say two things, with a proof of exactly what that buys. The record is theirs. The toll isn't in
their picture.
\item Ghost state (Owicki and Gries 1976; Filliâtre, Gondelman and Paskevich 2014): variables
added for a proof that can be erased without changing what the program computes. The flag and
the counter are ghost in that sense. The small machine's table of checked facts isn't, because
the step reads it and refuses on it, for every program.
\item Verifiable computation and proof-carrying data (Parno, Howell, Gentry and Raykova 2013;
Chiesa and Tromer 2010): a short proof, checkable by a stranger, that a computation's output is
right. That establishes the claim for the stranger, and anyone with the inputs can make one,
the copier included. The record here is about one machine's own history. A proof like that is
one way to carry the local record out of the box.
\item Commitments and entitlements (Brandom 1994): talk as score-keeping, tracking who is
committed to what and who is entitled to it. Brandom's entitlements can be defeated and can be
passed on by testimony. Here entitlement is priced, step by step, and a copy carries it only as
far as the copy is genuine. Same words, different machinery.
\item Correspondence assertions (Woo and Lam 1993; Gordon and Jeffrey 2003). In protocol
verification, ``this end event must be preceded by a begin event for the same thing'' is a
correspondence assertion, and a type system can prove a protocol keeps it against any
opponent. ``No certificate without an earlier commit, and no commit without an earlier check
of the same claim'' has that shape. Theirs is proved about programs by typing. Mine is enforced
by the step, which is the LCF comparison again, at the level of single instructions.
\item Monotonic parallel composition (\AA{}man Pohjola and Parrow 2016) and persistence in the
pi-calculus (Palamidessi, Saraswat, Valencia and Victor 2006). \AA{}man Pohjola and Parrow
separate models where a neighbouring process can never stop a step from models that can solve
consensus, and test-and-set, an instruction that writes a cell and reports what was there, is
on the consensus side. Palamidessi and coauthors compare messages that are used up with
messages that stay, and the end of their study is a reduction from Minsky machines. The small
machine, put into \AA{}man Pohjola and Parrow's setting with threads pooled on one shared state,
has a consensus process in their sense, so it lands on their non-monotonic side; that, and their
theorem, are checked (\code{cs\_machine\_not\_monotonic}, ConsensusSeparation.v). The
comparison with Palamidessi and coauthors' persistence stays a comparison.
\item Constructor theory (Deutsch and Marletto 2015; Marletto 2016; Marletto, Vedral and
coauthors 2022; Violaris and Marletto 2022): physics as which tasks a device that runs in a
cycle can do, with irreversible meaning possible while the inverse isn't. It's the nearest
physics I know to ``committing costs'' stated for a cycling device. It's quantum and
approximate, and the chairs are classical, finite and exact
(Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}).
\item Accountability and fault detection (Haeberlen and Kuznetsov 2009; K\"usters, Truderung
and Vogt 2010; Treitz and K\"unnemann 2026): when an outside party can tell who misbehaved.
Their windows are this book's windows, and the trust they leave over, an honest log or a
trusted step, is left over here too (Section~\ref{sec:windows}).
\item Transferability (Easwaran 2009): a deductive proof lets you learn from someone you don't
trust because you can check every step, and a probabilistic proof doesn't. That's the argument
for carrying the object and re-running the check (Section~\ref{sec:windows}).
\end{itemize}

[3] The smallest piece
This is textbook mathematics (Halmos, section 7), and the book uses it as a foundation. I'm
claiming nothing new in it. Checked as \code{nec\_t\_rel\_of\_partition\_equiv},
\code{nec\_t\_blocks\_of\_equiv\_partition}, \code{nec\_t\_rel\_round\_trip} and
\code{nec\_t\_blocks\_round\_trip} (NecTPartition.v).

[4] The smallest thing that could carry the bill
Defined as the record \code{CertificationSystem} (UniversalCertificationCost.v).

[5] The smallest thing that could carry the bill
Where to check it: the record's six fields are \code{cs\_state}, \code{cs\_instr}, \code{cs\_step}, \code{cs\_cost},
\code{cs\_cert}, and the toll \code{cs\_cert\_costs}; the theorem is \code{universal\_nfi\_any\_substrate}, in the
same file; and the checked proof is the induction above, step for step. That name says ``any
substrate,'' and it says too much: what the theorem quantifies over is every certification
system, a substrate with the toll built in as a field, so it's about every system that has the
toll and about no other. The same theorem under a name that says so is
\code{every\_toll\_system\_pays\_the\_floor}, in the same file; the old name stays because other
files use it. One honest edge:
the theorem does not assume that a YES state exists. If no state ever
reads YES, no run meets the theorem's hypothesis, so it's true of every run and tells you
nothing about any of them, which is a mathematician's way of saying
it's true and useless. The toy on your paper has a YES box, so it isn't useless there. It
would be natural to assume the system has to have at least one NO state and one YES state;
the checked statement has no such clause, and that's on purpose, because a theorem that
applies to no run is still a theorem and the toy is where you supply the runs.

[6] The smallest thing that could carry the bill
The theorem also runs backwards, and that direction is one line. Suppose every run from a state
reading no to a state reading yes costs at least one, whichever state you start from. A single
move is a run of length one, so a move that takes no to yes costs at least one, which is the
toll. The floor from every start and the toll are the same rule (\code{nec\_f\_floor\_iff\_a2}),
and nobody has to choose between them. From one fixed start that reads no, the floor asks for
the toll only at
the crossings you can reach without paying anything (\code{nec\_f\_floor\_at\_iff\_free\_reach});
a crossing you can only reach by paying has already cost at least one by the time you get there.

[7] The smallest thing that could carry the bill
And the bound of one is the smallest case of a count. Under the toll a run costs at least the number
of times it takes the reading from no to yes, with no permanence assumed
(\code{nec\_f\_cost\_ge\_rises}). That bound for every run is again the same rule as the toll
(\code{nec\_f\_rises\_iff\_a2}). And it is reached for every count: on a one-bit machine whose set
costs one and whose reset is free, setting and resetting $k$ times from no takes the reading
from no to yes $k$ times and costs
exactly $k$ (\code{nec\_f\_bit\_rises\_exact}). All of these are in NecFFloor.v.

[8] A closed room with no spare chair
Defined as \code{finite\_states}, \code{permanent} and \code{step\_injective} (PermanentCertification.v).

[9] A closed room with no spare chair
Checked as \code{permanent\_flip\_is\_not\_injective} (PermanentCertification.v). The proof there
uses the standard-library fact that a duplicate-free list of $n$ members inside a list of
$n$ is the whole list, which is the pigeonhole in the form Leurechon would recognise.

[10] Now charge for forgetting, and the toll falls out
\begin{premise}[Merge pricing]\label{prem:merge-pricing}
Every move that merges costs at least one: for all $i$, if $i$ is not injective then
$\mathrm{cost}(i) \ge 1$. (Checked name: \code{merging\_steps\_priced}.)
\end{premise}

[11] Now charge for forgetting, and the toll falls out
Checked as \code{a2\_from\_merging\_price\_and\_permanence}; the packaging of such a machine as a
Thiele machine is \code{certification\_system\_from\_merging\_price}, and the run-level floor
for it is \code{permanent\_certification\_trace\_floor} (PermanentCertification.v).

[12] Now charge for forgetting, and the toll falls out
The theorem asks for more than it uses. Only the yes-states need to be finitely many: with a
permanent reading and finitely many states reading yes, the rest of the machine as large as you
like, merge pricing still gives the toll (\code{nec\_f\_toll\_from\_merges\_yes\_finite}). And merge
pricing asks for more than the toll needs. Under those same two conditions the toll holds exactly
when every move that flips the reading somewhere and merges is priced
(\code{nec\_f\_a2\_iff\_flipping\_merges\_priced}). Merge pricing gives that
(\code{nec\_f\_merging\_priced\_weakens}), and the eight-state machine described later in this
section, with its jump made free, meets the toll without merge pricing
(\code{nec\_f\_a2\_without\_merge\_pricing}). All four are in NecFMerge.v.

[13] Now charge for forgetting, and the toll falls out
One more way to see the same thing, because it answers a rival I'd otherwise owe you. The
rival says: leave certification alone. Price \emph{erasure}, and keep an honest flag that reports
when something was erased. Fine; but a system can set its certificate at cost zero with its
erasure flag reporting nothing, exactly because the certifying move merged two states and
the flag didn't say so (\code{commit\_without\_erasure\_system\_is\_not\_honest}). Make the erasure
flag honest, so that it reports every move that forgets, and on a finite machine with a
flag that stays up the erasure law sends the same bill the toll does
(\code{honest\_erasure\_accounting\_implies\_a2}). The toll isn't a rival to
honest erasure accounting. It's what honest erasure accounting says about the certifying
move.

[14] Door one: keep everything, and nothing ever has to merge
Take a strip of paper that never runs out of boxes. The machine's whole state is the strip.
Each move writes a 1 in the next empty box, and the flag is up as soon as any box has a 1 in
it.
Check the three premises. Permanent: once there's a 1, there's always a 1. Flip: the empty
strip reads NO, and after one move it reads YES. Forgets nothing: every move can be undone by
erasing the last 1, so two different strips never become the same strip. So here is a machine
that raises a flag, keeps it up, and never merges anything. The room argument has no grip on
it, and the toll doesn't follow. The checked version is \code{unbounded\_history\_escapes}, and its
state is a list of bits of any length, which is the formal way of saying the strip has no end.

[15] Door one: keep everything, and nothing ever has to merge
There are three separate claims here, and I need to keep them apart. The
theorem shows the strip machine has the other two properties and forgets nothing. The sentence
``and this machine is infinite'' is a second theorem, \code{nec\_f\_history\_infinite}
(NecFPaperToys.v): no finite list holds every state of the strip. The first theorem contains no
cost at all. ``Certifies for free'' is written in a second theorem, \code{history\_escape}
(AxMerge.v): there the strip is counted as a number, each move adds one at a cost of zero,
nothing merges, merge pricing holds, and the toll fails. So the words and the files line up, in three theorems.

[16] Door two: let the flag come back down
Take one coin. The rule is: turn it over. Heads is YES, tails is NO. Every move is undoable
(turn it over again), so nothing is forgotten. Tails goes to heads: that's a flip, NO to YES.
And heads goes to tails: the same move takes the flag back down somewhere else. Two states,
finite as it gets, and the move that raises the flag forgets nothing, so a schedule that
charges only for forgetting is allowed to charge it nothing. The file states that the one move
forgets nothing
(it is injective) and its on-then-off behaviour (\code{revocable\_certificate\_escapes}); that the coin is
finite and that the price may be zero are written in \code{toggle\_escape} (AxMerge.v), which
prices the turn at zero, meets merge pricing, and fails the toll.
The thing that walked out the door is permanence.

[17] Door two: let the flag come back down
Checked as \code{flip\_merges\_or\_revokes}, with the single-move form of the room argument as
\code{permanent\_at\_flip\_is\_not\_injective} (PermanentRecordPricing.v).

[18] Door two: let the flag come back down
Defined as \code{forced\_priced} (PermanentRecordPricing.v).

[19] Door two: let the flag come back down
Checked as \code{forced\_priced\_iff\_merges} (PermanentRecordPricing.v).

[20] Door two: let the flag come back down
Checked as \code{forced\_priced\_iff\_merges}, \code{permanent\_record\_write\_is\_forced\_priced}, and the
three-card counterexample \code{forced\_price\_without\_permanent\_record} (PermanentRecordPricing.v).

[21] Door three: forget for free
The third door is the easiest to describe and the hardest to argue about, so I'll describe
it and then spend the rest of the section on the argument. Take two sheets of paper, one
blank and one stamped. One move: stamp whatever you're handed. Finite, two states.
Permanent: stamped stays stamped. Flip: blank goes to stamped. Forgets: both sheets end up as
the same stamped sheet. Everything the room argument needs is here, and the move merges. Now
set the price of that move to zero. The toll fails, and you can watch it fail: the flag went
up for nothing.
That's \code{free\_merge\_escapes} (PermanentCertification.v), and what walked out the door is the charge on forgetting. (The
same file shows that a price of one on that move satisfies all three premises,
\code{priced\_reset\_satisfies\_premises}, so the premises can hold together; door three is about
whether they must.) Nothing in counting stops you from setting that price to zero. Only a
claim about the world does. The nearest such claim is Landauer's, and I have to be exact
about the gap between the two: the premise as written charges \emph{every} forgetting move,
whether or not you knew which state you were in, and Landauer's own heat, as the coin under
the cup below will show, is owed only for what you didn't know. So the premise is Landauer's
claim taken in its worst case over what the machine might be holding, and that's the
version the counting needs.

[22] How much: the price grows with the squeeze
Back to door three's stamp, with four sheets this time: three blank, one stamped, and the
stamp-everything move. Four states go to one. Before the move, to say which sheet you had took
two yes-or-no questions (is it one of the first two? then which of those two?); after the
move, it takes none. Two bits gone. Two halvings. So that move costs at least two marks, and
two marks is enough: a cost of exactly two satisfies the halving rule (both facts are checked
for these four sheets, \code{quad\_stamp\_needs\_two} and \code{quad\_stamp\_cost\_two\_is\_priced}). The
general statement is m + k at most 2 to the cost times m, and next to it in the file sits a
logarithm form that rounds both logarithms up and is weaker for it (three states and one
newcomer round to the same two bits on both sides, and the rounded form says nothing). The
halving premise, like the one before it, is a definition used as an assumption. The units
are halvings. To turn a halving into joules of heat you multiply by $k_B T \ln 2$, and that
multiplication is physics; the counting stops before it.

[23] How much: the price grows with the squeeze
\begin{premise}[Halving price]\label{prem:halving}
Each mark pays for at most one halving: for every move $i$ and every list $D$ of states with
no repeats, $|D| \le 2^{\mathrm{cost}(i)} \cdot \mathrm{img}_i(D)$. (Checked name:
\code{compression\_priced}. It implies merge pricing, since a merge sends two different states to
one and so needs $\mathrm{cost}(i) \ge 1$.)
\end{premise}

[24] How much: the price grows with the squeeze
The four sheets are the case $m = 1$, $k = 3$, where the bound is reached. With
$k = 1$ the bound says $\mathrm{cost}(i) \ge 1$, which is the toll again
(\code{a2\_from\_compression\_price\_and\_permanence}). Checked as
\code{permanent\_flips\_compression\_bound}, with the rounded-logarithm form
\code{permanent\_flips\_log\_bound} and the counting-only cousin \code{permanent\_flips\_collapse\_at\_least}
(at least $k$ states collapse, with no price premise at all).

[25] How much: the price grows with the squeeze
The bound is reached for every $m \ge 1$ and every cost $c$. Whenever $m + k \le 2^c m$, a finite
machine with a permanent reading and the halving price has $m$ yes-states and a move of cost
exactly $c$ that turns a listed $k$ distinct no-states to yes. The move turns every other no-state
of that machine to yes as well, $(2^c - 1)m$ of them in all. No cost meeting the halving price on
that machine charges the move less than $c$ (\code{nec\_f\_squeeze\_tight\_every\_k} and
\code{nec\_f\_grid\_cost\_least}, NecFSqueeze.v). So the least whole-number cost that meets the bound
is exactly $\lceil \log_2 \frac{m+k}{m} \rceil$ (\code{nec\_f\_least\_cost\_exists} and
\code{nec\_f\_least\_cost\_is\_ceiling}, NecFSqueezeLog.v).

[26] How much: the price grows with the squeeze
Four more facts about the halving price, and the last one is a limit. First, the plainer
reading above is a theorem on a finite machine: the halving price holds exactly when no move has
a landing spot with more than $2^{\mathrm{cost}(i)}$ states landing on it
(\code{ent2\_compression\_priced\_iff\_fibres}). Second, for the eight-state machine
described later in this section that's a theorem about its own price list: no
move has a larger pile-up than $2^{\mathrm{cost}}$, so the halving price holds, and the toll follows
from it (\code{ent2\_frag\_compression\_priced}, \code{ent2\_frag\_toll\_by\_compression}); and the price
list is the least that meets the halving price, since any price list that does charges every move at
least what this one charges (\code{ent2\_frag\_cost\_minimal}). Third, the squeeze bound is met with
equality there: its stamp turns on the four states whose flag is down, beside the four whose flag is already up, and
$4 + 4 = 2^{1} \cdot 4$ (\code{ent2\_frag\_bound\_attained}). Fourth, and this is the limit, the small
machine of Part IV as a whole does not meet the halving price, whatever procedure you use to decide
whether two of its states are equal, because one of its free moves, the one that takes one off
a number the machine holds, lands two different states of the whole machine on the same
state (\code{ent2\_small\_not\_compression\_priced}; the pair is in
Theorem~\ref{thm:frag-merge-split}). That the halving premise fails there is a theorem
(CompressionSmall2.v). What does hold there is the same premise asked of the record layer alone,
and it gives the toll on that machine too (Theorem~\ref{thm:record-merge-toll}, below).

[27] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{step\_entropy\_invariant\_if\_injective} (PermanentCertificationEntropy.v). That's
why a price on removed bits can leave every reversible move free, which is the shape the
toll needs: the bill lands only where something was forgotten.

[28] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{permanent\_step\_entropy\_ceiling} and, for the even spread, \code{permanent\_flip\_uniform\_entropy\_drop} (PermanentCertificationEntropy.v).

[29] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
\begin{premise}[Landauer's principle, as a premise]\label{prem:landauer}
A step that lowers the entropy of the machine's state by $\Delta H$ bits, at temperature
$T$, gives off heat $Q \ge k_B T \ln 2 \cdot \Delta H$, where $k_B$ is Boltzmann's constant.
(Checked name: \code{landauer\_heat}, with $k_B T$ as one variable.)
\end{premise}

[30] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{permanent\_step\_entropy\_ceiling}, \code{permanent\_flip\_uniform\_entropy\_drop},
\code{entropy\_le\_log\_support}, \code{permanent\_flip\_heat\_floor}, and \code{permanent\_flip\_heat\_positive}
(PermanentCertificationEntropy.v).

[31] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
How big is that? Take the case where the squeeze is two, one state flipping in beside one that
was already yes, so $m = k = 1$ and the floor is $k_B T \ln 2$. At room temperature, about 300
kelvin, that's roughly three zeptojoules: three thousandths of a billionth of a billionth of a
joule. And that's the \emph{biggest} bill a single flip gets from this corollary. With $k = 1$ the floor is $k_B T \ln \frac{m+1}{m}$, and it shrinks as more states
already read yes: with a thousand of them it's about a thousandth of $k_B T$, some seven
hundred times less than the case of two. So, for an even spread over the states in play, one
mark is not one halving. It's one halving exactly when the squeeze is two; for any thinner
squeeze and an even spread, the physics asks for less than a halving, and the toll still charges
a whole mark, because marks come in whole numbers (that's the rounding the entropy road to the
toll leans on, a little further down). Read in the worst case over what the machine might be
holding, the way the halving price reads a move, it's at least a full bit every time. A permanent flip
merges two states (Theorem~\ref{thm:perm-flip-merges}); put half the chance on each, and the move
removes a full bit, whatever $m$ is. Checked as \code{merge\_pair\_removes\_a\_bit} and
\code{permanent\_flip\_spread\_loses\_a\_bit} (PermanentCertificationEntropy.v). So the gap
between the mark and the bits is a fact about the even spread. In the worst case the flip removes
at least the full bit the mark pays for. Whatever the size,
it's a bill only if you grant the premise; nothing on this page measured anything. And even
granted, it's a bill nobody will ever see on a meter: real logic gates spend many thousands of
times more than this on every switch, even the frugal ones. One 2023 tally of the numbers puts
switching a single CMOS gate at a hundred attojoules at the low end, over thirty thousand
times this. The point of the number isn't that
certifying is expensive. It's that, on the premise, it isn't zero.

[32] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{permanent\_flip\_full\_support\_entropy\_drop\_positive} and
\code{permanent\_flip\_full\_support\_heat\_positive} (PermanentCertificationEntropy.v).

[33] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{a2\_from\_entropy\_price\_and\_permanence}, with the packaging
\code{certification\_system\_from\_entropy\_price} and the run-level floor
\code{entropy\_priced\_trace\_floor} (PermanentCertificationEntropy.v).

[34] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{entropy\_drop\_le\_log\_fibre}, \code{entropy\_drop\_of\_fibre},
\code{worst\_case\_entropy\_drop}, \code{entropy\_priced\_iff\_compression\_priced},
\code{merging\_priced\_iff\_pair\_halving}, \code{merging\_priced\_iff\_pair\_entropy} and
\code{one\_premise\_three\_forms} (PricingOnePremise.v). So the premise under all three roads is
one premise, Landauer's in its worst case over what the machine might hold, and I wrote it three
ways because each way makes a different theorem short. Merge pricing asks it only about pairs,
which is why that road gives the toll and stops there; the squeeze needs the whole premise.

[35] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{known\_state\_step\_removes\_no\_entropy} and \code{known\_state\_flip\_forces\_no\_heat} (PermanentCertificationEntropy.v).

[36] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
A deterministic run from a known start has a known state at every step. For such a run, the
adopted entropy-loss premise reduces to $Q \ge 0$: it supplies no positive heat lower bound.
That does not establish that a particular device attains zero heat. The chairs guarantee a
collision among the already-yes states together with the entering no-state. They do not
guarantee that the entering state takes part in the collision (a four-state machine where it
doesn't is \code{nec\_f\_collision\_avoids\_s}, NecFMerge.v). For an input distribution
supported only on no-states, this counting argument alone therefore forces no positive entropy
loss. Whether other states in that support merge depends on the move. The operation-level
ledger toll still follows under merge pricing.

[37] The same bill, written in bits, and when it's zero
Checked as \code{entropy\_drop\_as\_point\_sum}, \code{entropy\_drop\_nonneg}, and
\code{entropy\_drop\_pos\_of\_support\_merge} (PermanentCertificationEntropy.v).

[38] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
A finite piece can still sit inside, and here's the smallest one I could build where every
premise is a theorem. None of them is left as a hypothesis. Four slots times a flag, eight states. Three
moves: stamp (set the flag, go to the next slot, cost one), jump to slot $a$ (cost two), next slot
(cost zero). It's finite (\code{fin\_finite}) and permanent (\code{fin\_permanent}); the next-slot
move forgets nothing (\code{fnext\_injective}); the stamp and the jump merge
(\code{fcertify\_merges}, \code{fjump\_merges}); merges are priced
(\code{fin\_merging\_priced}), and priced per halving (\code{fin\_compression\_priced}). So this
section's two conclusions hold on it outright (\code{fin\_a2\_from\_merging\_price},
\code{fin\_a2\_from\_compression\_price}, FiniteCertMachine.v). The counting lemma underneath,
that a duplicate-free list with at most $K$ preimages per image has at most $K$ times as many
members as images, is proved generically (\code{fiber\_bound\_compression}). Merge pricing alone
would take any price of at least one for the jump. The jump's price of
two is what the halving price asks, since the jump squeezes four states into one. One more fact
about it, also checked: each move's price is exactly the number of halvings in its largest
squeeze. For a move that costs $c$, no state is landed on by more than $2^c$ states, and some
state is landed on by exactly that many (stamp squeezes at most two states into one, jump to a
slot squeezes four, and next-slot squeezes nothing).

[39] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
Checked as \code{frag\_small\_certify\_merges} and \code{frag\_small\_not\_merge\_priced}
(FragmentSmall.v), and \code{rm\_commit\_merges}, \code{rm\_merge\_price\_charges\_record\_moves},
\code{rm\_toll\_from\_any\_merge\_price}, \code{rm\_cert\_system\_from\_merge\_price},
\code{rm\_merge\_price\_gives\_record\_price}, \code{rm\_certify\_record\_merge},
\code{rm\_commit\_record\_merge}, \code{rm\_base\_moves\_no\_record\_merge},
\code{rm\_small\_record\_priced}, \code{rm\_toll\_from\_record\_price},
\code{rm\_cert\_system\_from\_record\_price} and \code{rm\_record\_price\_and\_exact\_toll}
(RecordMerge.v).

[40] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
One piece of it I can hand you. Take
one move and repeat it from one start, forever. On a finite machine the states that run passes through are
finitely many, so it falls into a loop. If the move forgot nothing on those states, it would
be a reshuffle of them, and a reshuffle brings every one of them back around, the start
included, where the flag was down. So if the flag goes up along that run and stays up from
some point on, the move merges two states \emph{the run itself visits}. The shared chair is
one the machine actually sits in. And it doesn't touch the known-state point above: a merge
among states the run visits still costs Landauer nothing if the run knew where it was and
something outside timed the step. Checked as \code{nec\_f\_orbit\_merges\_visited} and
\code{nec\_f\_repeat\_merges\_visited} (NecFRepeat.v), for any map iterated from a start whose flag
is down, with the flag up at every step from some point on; no permanence premise is used.

[41] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
Runs that mix different moves split two ways, and the answer is short enough to give here. If the moves are picked from outside, in any order at
all, the answer is no. Take two states, one reading no and one reading yes. Move $a$ swaps them;
move $b$ does nothing. Run $a$ once and then $b$ forever: the flag goes up at the first step and
stays up for good, and neither move merges anything anywhere. The swap would take the flag down
from the yes-state if it ever ran there; on this run it never does, and that's exactly the
weakened permanence this paragraph is about. But if the machine is closed, picking its next
move from its own state, the run is the orbit of one map, the step with whatever move the state
picks, and the single-move argument applies to that map as it
stands. Now say the moves come in a
pattern that eventually repeats, one word $w$ of moves over and over. Suppose the flag is down at some step after the
repeating starts, and goes up and stays up after that. Count the repetitions from a step where the flag was down (start
$w$ at that letter if you have to) and run the same argument on the composite of $w$'s moves, at the states where such a repetition begins. It merges two
of those states, both visited; follow the two through the letters of $w$, and the first letter
where their paths meet merges two states the run visits at that letter's place in the pattern.
So: moves picked from outside, no; a closed machine or a repeating program, yes
(\code{nec\_f\_outside\_moves\_no\_merge}, \code{nec\_f\_closed\_merges\_visited} and
\code{nec\_f\_word\_merges\_visited}, NecFRepeat.v; the last finds the letter and the two
repetitions, from a flag down at the start and up at every repetition from some point on).

[42] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
And one limited piece of the chances-and-time-spans version, set in continuous time and checked
in all but one step,
because it shows exactly where the known-state escape stops. Take a device with no outside hand
at all: a little random process hopping among finitely many states at rates that never change,
with the usual physical conditions (every hop has a reverse hop, and the rates obey local
detailed balance), able to get from any state to any other. Left to run, it settles into a
long-run spread $\pi$ and spends stretches with the flag up and stretches with it down; call
their mean lengths $T_{\mathrm{up}}$ and $T_{\mathrm{down}}$. Start it in a no-state you know.
Then the entropy it produces from then on is at least $\ln\bigl(1 + T_{\mathrm{up}}/T_{\mathrm{down}}\bigr)$,
in units of $k_B$. The argument is three lines once the pieces have names. With fixed rates, the
entropy produced is at least the drop in relative entropy between the device's spread and
$\pi$; that's half of Massimiliano Esposito and Christian Van den Broeck's split of entropy
production (2010). A start known to be in state $s$ has relative entropy $-\ln\pi(s)$, which is
at least $-\ln\pi(\mathrm{no})$, where $\pi(\mathrm{no})$ is the long-run chance of the flag being
down, and the relative entropy runs down to zero as the device settles. And the long-run
chances of up and down stand in the ratio of the mean stretch lengths, because the crossings up
and the crossings down balance, so $-\ln\pi(\mathrm{no}) = \ln\bigl(1 + T_{\mathrm{up}}/T_{\mathrm{down}}\bigr)$.
Read it this way: each doubling of how long the record stays up, against how long it sits down,
costs about one more $k_B\ln 2$, and a record that stays up forever on a fixed-rate device costs
without bound. That's Murashita, Funo and Ueda's absolute irreversibility landing on the flip
at last, with $1-\lambda$ the long-run chance of the starting state. It isn't new physics:
nothing in it needs a record, since an autonomous reset to a fixed state obeys the same bound,
and the time-symmetric-control result above covers the same ground with an error rate in place
of the time ratio. And it isn't the theorem I said I didn't have. It's a first honest step
toward it, and it pins down where ``the premise sets no heat floor for a known state'' needs its outside clock.
Hold that argument to what it says. The entropy is added up from the moment the device sits in
the known no-state $s$ until it has settled, over one whole relaxation. The starting spread is
all on $s$, and $\pi$ is the device's long-run spread. The inequality rests on fixed rates,
finitely many states, every state reachable from every other, a reverse for every hop, and local
detailed balance to read the entropy as heat. It's one bound on one relaxation, and it doesn't
charge each certification again. It also isn't the permanence of the finite-state theorems on a
noisy device: on this device the flag comes back down, and $T_{\mathrm{up}}$ and
$T_{\mathrm{down}}$ are average stretches. The discrete-time core is checked, for a finite chain
whose steady spread $\pi$ is positive and satisfies detailed balance (RelaxationEntropy.v): the
entropy produced in one step is exactly the drop in relative entropy to $\pi$, and never negative
(\code{re\_sigma\_is\_drop} and \code{re\_sigma\_nonneg}); from a known start $s$ the entropy produced
in the first $T$ steps is $-\ln\pi(s)$ minus the relative entropy left at step $T$, so it never
exceeds $-\ln\pi(s)$ and reaches it in the limit exactly when that remainder goes to zero
(\code{re\_known\_start\_total} and \code{re\_known\_start\_bound}); $-\ln\pi(s) \ge -\ln\pi(\mathrm{no})$
for a start among the no-states (\code{re\_known\_start\_vs\_set}); the flow from the no-states to the
yes-states equals the flow back (\code{re\_flux\_balance}); and with each mean stretch taken as its
mass over that flow, $-\ln\pi(\mathrm{no}) = \ln(1 + T_{\mathrm{up}}/T_{\mathrm{down}})$
(\code{re\_stretch\_ratio}).

[43] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
Settling first. A chain doesn't have to settle. It can get stuck in a corner, or cycle round
forever. What rules that out here is a condition named for Wolfgang Doeblin, a German-born French
mathematician, son of the novelist Alfred Döblin, who took his doctorate at the Sorbonne in 1938
for work on chains like this one and died in June 1940, at twenty-five. In words: from wherever
the device is, its next step lands on each state with at least some fixed fraction $\delta$ of
that state's long-run chance. No state is ever out of reach in one step, and none is favoured
so heavily that the others starve. Under that condition each step shrinks the distance between
where the device is and where it's going by the factor $1-\delta$, so the distance dies off
geometrically and the spread converges to $\pi$ at every state (\code{rc\_contract} and
\code{rc\_converges}, RelaxationConvergence.v). Here's the payoff for the bound. The entropy
produced from the known start stays under $-\ln\pi(s)$, and it also gets there: after $T$ steps
it falls short by at most $(4/m)(1-\delta)^{2T}$, where $m$ is the smallest long-run chance of
any state (\code{rc\_known\_start\_gap} and \code{rc\_known\_start\_limit}). So $-\ln\pi(s)$ is the
exact price of the whole relaxation, and the bound is reached.

[44] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
The same story holds when the device hops at random moments, at fixed rates, with no clock
ticking. At every instant the relative entropy to $\pi$ falls at exactly the rate entropy is
being produced, and that rate is never negative (\code{rcd\_D\_derivative},
\code{rcd\_sigma\_nonneg} and \code{rcd\_D\_monotone}, RelaxationContinuous.v); add the rate up
between two times and you get exactly the drop in relative entropy between them
(\code{rcd\_produced\_window}). For settling, Doeblin's condition becomes a condition on rates:
the rate of hopping from any state $x$ to any state $y$ is at least $c$ times $\pi(y)$, for some
fixed $c > 0$. Then entropy is always being produced at least $c$ times as fast as the relative
entropy still left, and that forces the relative entropy down at least as fast as $e^{-ct}$
(\code{rcl\_sigma\_ge} and \code{rcl\_D\_decay}, RelaxationContinuousLimit.v). One wrinkle needed
its own proof. A known start puts all its weight on one state and none anywhere else, and the
logarithms in the relative entropy go bad at zero. The check shows the relative entropy is still
continuous at the starting instant, because $u \ln u$ goes to zero as $u$ does
(\code{rcl\_D\_at\_zero}). So adding up the production from the start out to infinity gives
exactly $-\ln\pi(s)$ (\code{rcl\_known\_start\_total}).

[45] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
Then the stretches. The bound used the fact that the long-run chances of up and down stand in the
ratio of the mean stretch lengths, and I took the mean length of a stretch with the flag up to be
the long-run weight of the up-states divided by the flow into them. That's a theorem. Here's the argument. Start each stretch the way the flow starts them, count the steps until the
chain leaves the up-states, and the average is exactly $\pi(\mathrm{up})$ over the flow, again
under Doeblin's condition (\code{rs\_mean\_stretch}, RelaxationStretch.v). The proof is a
telescoping sum: the long-run weight that has stayed up for $k$ steps splits into the stretches
that started just now and the weight that stays up one step more.

[46] What you're entitled to, and what you're not
With the stretches measured in seconds, when the device hops at random moments, it's checked
too, and the route is different. Ask, for each up-state, how long on average the device takes to
leave the up-states from there. Call that its leaving time. The leaving times obey one plain
rule: from any up-state, the rate out of it times its leaving time, minus the rates into the other
up-states times their leaving times, comes to exactly one second. Average the leaving times over
the way the flow starts stretches, multiply by the flow, and the rule plus the balance of the
steady spread (as much weight flows into each state as out of it) rearrange the sum into exactly
$\pi(\mathrm{up})$ (\code{rsc\_mass\_over\_flow}, RelaxationStretchContinuous.v). That the
rule has a solution at all needs proving, since nothing promises it in advance, and Doeblin's
condition supplies it: run a clock that ticks faster than any rate in the chain, and the leaving
times are the limit of a count that only grows and never passes one over $c\,\pi(\mathrm{down})$
(\code{rsc\_leave\_exists}). Last, the seconds themselves. Take the chance that a stretch started
by the flow is still running at time $t$, with the chain stopped the moment it leaves the
up-states. That chance starts at one and falls at least as fast as $e^{-c\,\pi(\mathrm{down})\,t}$
(\code{rsc\_S\_decay}). The time spent up, which is that chance added up over time, comes out to
the averaged leaving time minus what's still running, and as time runs on it closes in on
$\pi(\mathrm{up})$ over the flow (\code{rsc\_mean\_stretch\_time} and
\code{rsc\_stretch\_identity}). Mass over flow, in seconds. The bound's two premises are both
checked now, in steps and in seconds.

[47] Two different things called ``narrowing''
Checked as \code{run\_narrowing\_priced\_log} (KnowledgeNarrowing.v). The run itself is the witness;
nothing else is handed in. That will matter in Section~\ref{sec:entitlement}, and I'll say why at the end of this
section.

[48] The demon on a sheet of paper
Defined as \code{knowledge} (KnowledgeNarrowing.v).

[49] The demon on a sheet of paper
Checked as \code{observer\_narrowing\_can\_be\_free}, \code{demon\_observer\_learns},
\code{demon\_machine\_spread\_kept}, \code{measure\_forgets\_nothing}, \code{wipe\_merges}, and
\code{wipe\_costs\_at\_least\_one} (KnowledgeNarrowing.v).

[50] The demon on a sheet of paper
The demon is the picture. The smallest case is smaller. The claim as written already fails on a machine with
two states and every move free, because the observer's very first look tells the two apart, and it
never fails on a machine with one state (\code{nec\_f\_two\_state\_observer\_free},
\code{nec\_f\_one\_state\_observer\_priced}, NecFNarrowing.v). That's why the interesting version is
learning \emph{after} the first look, which is where the three states of
Theorem~\ref{thm:three-states} come in. And WIPE's price needs less than the halving rule: merge pricing
alone charges it at least one (\code{nec\_f\_wipe\_under\_merge\_pricing}).

[51] The smallest machine that teaches for free, and the one that can't
Checked as \code{free\_incremental\_narrowing\_with\_three} and
\code{no\_free\_incremental\_narrowing\_below\_three} (KnowledgeNarrowingMinimal.v), and the
demon-against-the-first-look form as \code{demon\_refutes\_incremental}.

[52] Only one honest count
Those two proofs are the whole of it, and they're about any counter machine. They're on
the page, where you can check every line. Their formal counterparts over arbitrary states are
\code{nec\_f\_conservation}, with \code{nec\_f\_counter\_monotone} for the climb, and
\code{nec\_f\_initiality} (NecFCounter.v). The machine I build later is one of these, and on
it the run form is a checked theorem: the ledger after any list of instructions is the start
plus the sum of their costs (Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-toll}). The uniqueness half holds even with
the books' rule asked only of reachable states (\code{nec\_f\_initiality\_reachable\_rule}).

[53] Only one honest count
Coming down from lists to the states of the machine running them is where it gets
interesting, and where the borrowed word stops. Two different lists can leave a machine in the
same state. If they leave the target in two different states, there's no single place
to send that state. That's the whole obstruction, and it's exact: the map from states exists
on the reachable ones precisely when equal states always come from lists that agree on the
other side. Call that the \emph{descent} condition, because it's what
lets the fold come down from lists to states. A toll doesn't force it. Take the one-bit machine whose set costs one and whose
reset is free. It obeys the toll, yet a single set and a reset followed by a set leave its
bit in the same state. A target that keeps its whole history as its state tells those two
runs apart, so nothing comes down. So the fold is unique on lists, and that's what the word ``initial''
earns. It doesn't make anybody's states initial among everything that obeys the toll, and I
won't say it does. The fold and the descent condition are checked over any machine: the fold
is the one such map (\code{nec\_f\_fold\_unique}), descent holds exactly when each reachable
state goes to one target state (\code{nec\_f\_descent\_iff\_functional}), and a map the fold
factors through gives descent (\code{nec\_f\_factor\_gives\_descent}), all in NecFCounter.v; the
history-keeping machine is the example that shows where descent fails
(\code{nec\_f\_descent\_fails\_for\_history}).

[54] The toll is an old trick with a new name
Now point it at the flag. Potential: one if the flag is down, zero if it's up. A step from
down to up drops the potential by one, so for the step's cost plus potential change to be
non-negative, the step has to cost at least one. That's the toll, exactly, and the two are the
same statement: the toll holds for a machine if and only if every step has non-negative
amortized cost under the flag potential (``amortized cost'' is just that sum, the step's cost
plus the change in potential). Then the universal floor from Section~\ref{sec:toll}, ``any run from NO to YES
paid at least one,'' is just the potential method's standard conclusion, the drop in
potential from one to zero, and every system with the five parts that obeys the toll is an
instance (the checked record carries the toll as a field, so that's every certification
system). Two more facts fall out of the same picture. Under the toll, a run raises the flag
at most as many times as its total cost; each raise uses up a unit and a unit isn't used
twice. And if you demand the upper-bound version too, that the potential before a step covers
its cost plus the potential after, then the two together hold exactly when raising the flag
costs exactly one, everything else costs zero, and the flag never comes down; that's the
sharpest version of
the schedule, and notice that Section~\ref{sec:toll}'s toy doesn't meet it: the toy's VOUCH move, played from
YES, makes a mark
although it raises nothing, which the floor allows and the exact version forbids. (These
two theorems let the price depend on the state as well as the move's name, which is wider
than Section~\ref{sec:toll}'s per-name cost; a per-name cost is a special case.) Two older tools sit beside
these, named for completeness: running a list of moves in a ``writer'' that accumulates a
number returns the final state and the run's cost (\code{run\_writer\_is\_run\_and\_cost}), and a
per-move schedule is a ``grade'' fixed before the run (\code{run\_graded\_is\_run}); neither
embeds those frameworks' full type systems, and the files say so. The writer is the move
the programming-language researchers Norman Danner and Daniel Licata make in their cost semantics, a translation that carries a cost alongside
a program's result (Danner, Licata and Ramyaa, ICFP 2015; Danner and Licata, \emph{Journal of
Functional Programming}, 2022). The grade is Shin-ya Katsumata's: a computation carries its resource
as a grade, and the grades compose as the computations do (``Parametric effect monads and
semantics of effect systems,'' POPL 2014). Dominic Orchard, Vilem Liepelt and Harley Eades put grades into the
types of a working language (``Quantitative program reasoning with graded modal types,'' ICFP
2019). And the potential method went into types before any of those: the computer scientists
Martin Hofmann and Steffen Jost (POPL 2003) gave a type system that attaches potential to data and
predicts, before a first-order functional program runs, how much heap it can use, which is where
type-based amortized analysis begins; the multivariate work of Jan Hoffmann, Klaus Aehlig and
Hofmann (2011) carried it further. Read against them, the counter is a grade carried in the state by the step rule. No type
carries it. And what does that difference even mean? A cost that lives in a type gets checked
before the program runs, by whoever checks the types; a cost that lives in the state gets
written during the run, by the step itself. Same number, different moment of truth.

[55] The toll is an old trick with a new name
So is mine an embedding of their frameworks, or something weaker? Half of it is exactly theirs,
and the other half is left out on purpose. The counter \emph{is} their writer: a step that moves
the rest of the state without looking at the counter, hands back its cost, and has the cost added
on is a computation in the writer over whole numbers with addition, and running a list of moves in
that writer gives back the final state and the total cost, nothing more and nothing less
(\code{run\_writer\_is\_run\_and\_cost}). Every system in this book whose step never reads its own
counter has that shape. What doesn't come along is the part of their frameworks that works before
anything runs: the type systems that carry grades, Danner and Licata's bounding relation and the
recurrences they extract for a program's cost. That isn't a hole waiting for a patch. It's the
moment-of-truth difference I just named. Putting the count in the step is choosing the other
moment, so the grade here is written by the step at run time and never checked ahead of it.

[56] The toll is an old trick with a new name
How close do the two moments come? Close enough to say exactly, in a limited form. Take a small structured language over a machine with this kind of counter:
sequencing, branches whose two arms carry the same grade, and paid acts each graded with their
charge, and grade every program the way Katsumata's discipline does, adding grades along the
program. On loop-free programs the two agree on the nose: on every run that doesn't trap, the
counter rises by exactly the grade. The argument is conservation set beside the grade rule; the
counter's rise is the sum of the charges along the run, and the grade rule adds the same charges
in the same order. Loops break the agreement, and in one direction only. A loop that runs a
paid
check once for every unit
in some register, where the check
always
passes and is charged one,
costs that register's value on
entry, and no fixed whole number is an exact grade for it; a grade that's a function of the
inputs, the kind cost-aware type theories like calf (Niu, Sterling, Grodin and Harper, 2022)
allow, gets exactness back. So the counter carries their semantics exactly, agrees with fixed
grades wherever a fixed grade can be exact, and where a run's cost depends on its data it holds
the exact number that a fixed grade can only bound. Checked as \code{nec\_f\_grade\_exact}, with
the small machine as an instance in \code{nec\_f\_grade\_exact\_small}, and for the loop
\code{nec\_f\_loop\_cost}, \code{nec\_f\_loop\_no\_fixed\_grade} and \code{nec\_f\_loop\_input\_grade}
(NecFGrade.v). What has no counterpart in the step is the
checking ahead of time: a type checker turning a program away before it runs.

[57] The toll is an old trick with a new name
Defined as \code{amortized\_nonneg} and \code{cert\_potential} (CostSemanticsComparison.v).

[58] The toll is an old trick with a new name
Checked as \code{a2\_iff\_nonnegative\_amortized\_cost}, \code{potential\_telescoping}, \code{nfi\_by\_potential},
and \code{certification\_system\_is\_potential\_method} (CostSemanticsComparison.v), with the
state-dependent-cost results \code{flips\_le\_cost} and \code{a2\_and\_aara\_iff\_exact}
(CostFrameworks.v).

[59] The whole fact, in one paragraph
Checked as \code{decoding\_requires\_fiber\_constancy} and \code{selected\_representatives\_give\_decoder}
(ObservationPolicy.v), for arbitrary types.

[60] The clerk with two photographs
Not if the window has the wrong kind of blind spot. Here's why, and it's the cube again.
Suppose there are
two steps that give the clerk the same before-photo and the same after-photo, and one of them
raised the flag and the other didn't. (The two-move toy above is exactly such a pair; so are
``a stamp goes onto a document inside a closed drawer'' and ``someone opens and closes the
drawer.'') The clerk has one bill for that pair of photographs. If the bill is at least one,
it overcharges the step that didn't raise the flag. If it's zero, it undercharges the step
that did. Any bill that never misses a raise bills something that wasn't a raise, and no
bill from those photographs is exact. The fix is the plain one: put the flag in the
photograph. If the window shows the reading, then ``one when the reading goes from down to
up, zero otherwise'' is exact, and such a window has no pair like the one above. That's enough.
It isn't the only way: a window can hide the flag and still have no colliding pair, in
which case a bill from the photographs can be exact; what ShadowPricing.v proves the other way is
only that a colliding pair rules exact pricing out. And ``exact'' here means rise-only pricing: at least
one on every step that raises the flag, nothing on any step that doesn't. That's all the
clerk's problem needs, and the bill ``one for a raise, zero otherwise'' meets it
(\code{step\_price\_is\_exact}). So it's exact about \emph{which} steps get charged. It isn't exact about \emph{how much}, and it
doesn't pick out one bill: ``two for a raise, zero
otherwise'' meets it too. Pinning the bill down takes one more demand, a ceiling: over any
run, the total never exceeds the number of raises. With the floor and that ceiling there's
exactly one bill left, one per raise and zero otherwise; that's an if and only if, proved for
any system whose bill is decided step by step (\code{exact\_commitment\_pricing\_characterization},
CommitmentPredicateAdequacy.v). These are stated for any states and any steps. The window version, the cube theorem with a
price in place of a decoder, comes next.

[61] The clerk with two photographs
Checked as \code{shadow\_floor\_overcharges}, \code{shadow\_cannot\_price\_exactly},
\code{window\_showing\_reading\_prices\_exactly}, and \code{window\_showing\_reading\_has\_no\_collision}
(ShadowPricing.v), for arbitrary types.

[62] The three escapes, and what each one costs
Checked, for any pair like $A$ and $B$, over arbitrary types of states, claims, transcripts and
explanation relations; nothing in it uses more about the two states than their claim values and
their shared transcript. The collision argument is \code{ver\_collision\_blocks}, the richer-transcript
one is \code{ver\_no\_factor} (a verifier sound and complete for the claim under an explanation relation
cannot be one that
only looks at the bare part, once two transcripts with the same bare part
are explained, one by a
state with the claim and one by a state without it), and the three escapes are
\code{ver\_full\_state\_escape}, \code{ver\_commitment\_escape} (under the contract, with
\code{ver\_honest\_meets\_contract} and \code{ver\_unchecked\_breaks\_contract} for the two relations),
and \code{ver\_response\_escape} (VerifierSmall.v). The cost $1$ in the definition above is not in
any of those statements; it is a number I wrote down, and no proof reads it.

[63] The three escapes, and what each one costs
\begin{proof}
Observation~\ref{thm:u34-tc-window}(2) gives a loaded state and two runs from it that end with the
same window, one with the record up and one with it down, and, from one clean loaded state, two runs with the same window, one whose ledger rose by at least 3 and
one made of free base moves that leaves the ledger where it started (that theorem's proof builds it; \code{complete\_two\_runs} states it).
 Both end states explain the same bare transcript. A verifier
complete for the claim accepts that transcript, since a state where the claim holds explains it;
a verifier sound for the claim then makes the claim true of every state that explains it,
including the one where it's false. That's (1), and (2) is the same with the ledger: the paid run's end state satisfies the claim, and the free run's doesn't. For (3) the
two runs are written out in the statement: three moves land on line 4 in both runs with both
counters at 0, and the same two-state argument applies.
\end{proof}

[64] The three escapes, and what each one costs
Checked as \code{ver\_complete\_no\_record\_verifier}, \code{ver\_complete\_no\_ledger\_verifier} and
\code{ver\_small\_no\_ledger\_verifier} (VerifierSmall.v), the first two from
\code{complete\_hides\_record} and \code{complete\_two\_runs} of ThieleCompleteWindow.v. Put with the
three escapes, the whole picture is one statement for every certified Thiele-complete machine,
\code{ver\_corollary}, and for the small machine \code{ver\_small\_corollary}: the bare-transcript
verifier doesn't exist; the collision survives into any richer transcript that keeps it, so no
verifier that only reads its bare part works either; a verifier on full states exists; one on the
bare transcript with a committed bit exists, sound and complete when the bit is honest, and the
unchecked bit breaks the contract; and one on a truthful response exists. The collision is a
premise of the richer-transcript half, and the contract is assumed; no fourth way is claimed to
be impossible, and no hardness is proved.

[65] The count, exactly
Checked as \code{decision\_tree\_leaves\_le\_pow2\_depth} and \code{decision\_tree\_log2\_up\_leaf\_bound}
(DecisionTreeBound.v). Both are stated over the tree type alone, a leaf or a branch, with
nothing about any machine in them.

[66] The count, exactly
For any system with a counter that obeys conservation it is two checked theorems, put together
by hand; nothing in Coq joins them. \code{nec\_e\_cs\_count\_any} (NecEEnt.v) proves it over any
Thiele machine with the sum of the charges of a run in place of the counter's rise, and
without needing $|\Omega'| > 0$; and \code{nec\_f\_conservation} (NecFCounter.v), stated over any
counter machine kept by a price list, says that sum is the counter's rise. A system that is both
gets the theorem. The bill form is also part of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ent-cs} below. The tree
half is the checked lemma above; the rest is arithmetic on three whole numbers, the two lengths
and the leaf count, and on the counter. Notice what's missing from the hypotheses: anything tying the
lists to the run. That's the honest shape of the bound.

[67] What you've got
Checked as \code{ent\_narrowing\_strengthens}, \code{ent\_cs\_count} and \code{ent\_cs\_entitlement}
(EntitlementSmall.v); the tree lemma underneath is also there, as \code{ent\_leaves\_le\_pow2} and
\code{ent\_log2\_leaves\_le\_depth}. Theorem~\ref{thm:u08-count} is the same count with the counter's
rise, so for a system that obeys conservation it's the special case. Three things in the hypotheses
feed nothing, and I'm naming them here so you don't have to find them: (H3) isn't used
by any of the three
conclusions; from (H1) only the inclusion is used, and the gap goes unused; and in (H2)
the words ``not in $\Omega'$'' go unused, since a candidate whose observation differs from every survivor's can't be a survivor itself. And (H4)'s $|\Omega'| > 0$ is used in that proof but can be dropped too: the covering forces it once the prior has a member (\code{nec\_e\_cs\_entitlement\_stronger}, NecEEnt.v).

[68] When the run does the narrowing
Checked as \code{ent\_cs\_run\_entitlement} (EntitlementSmall.v), with the counting underneath as
\code{ent\_stages\_product} and \code{ent\_stages\_share\_bits}. Part (3) is the exact count: take
base-two logarithms and $\log_2 |\Omega| - \log_2 |\Omega'|$ is the sum, over the tests the run
passed, of $\log_2 (b_i / a_i)$, the bits each one took. Part (2) answers the complaint I made
about the covering, that it doesn't say why anything was deleted: here everything deleted was
deleted by a test the run passed and paid for. Two premises stay, the prior list and the share.
The share is needed. On the small machine one CHECK of ``$A \ge 15$,'' asked of sixteen
candidates, keeps one, a $2^{-4}$ share, and narrows by 4 index bits on a run of 3 record moves
(Proposition~\ref{prop:ent-share-needed}); with $k = 1$ part (4) would say $4 \le 3$. The same
statement on every certified Thiele-complete machine, where the tests are the CHECK moves and the bill is
the ledger, is Theorem~\ref{thm:ent-run-machine}.

[70] What happens when a computation learns
\begin{remark}[What the toll prices, and what it doesn't]\label{rem:u09-three}
Read off Definition~\ref{def:certsys} and Observation~\ref{thm:universal-floor}, and checked on the
board as a Thiele machine (\code{nec\_f\_board\_raw\_free}, \code{nec\_f\_board\_floor} and
\code{nec\_f\_board\_floor\_flat}, NecFPaperToys.v). (1) The toll puts no bound on the cost of a non-raising
move; on Section~\ref{sec:toll}'s board STAY and WORK are non-raising moves and cost $0$. (2) Every
raise is charged at least $1$, by the toll, and every run from a state reading no to
one reading yes has total cost at least $1$, by the universal floor. (3) The price is flat:
the floor's proof uses nothing about the end state but its reading, so adding any hypothesis
about what else the end state shows leaves the bound at $1$ and cannot raise it. A system that
wants to charge more for a deeper certification has to write that into its price list, move
by move; the toll doesn't do it for anyone.
\end{remark}

[71] Test, then commit, and nothing forces the second to follow the first
\begin{remark}[Nothing makes the crossing wait]\label{rem:u09-no-wait}
Read off Definition~\ref{def:certsys}, and checked on the board as \code{nec\_f\_board\_no\_wait}
(NecFPaperToys.v). The toll constrains
$\mathrm{cost}(i)$ for moves that cross, and nothing else; the step function is any function
of the current state and the move. So a Thiele machine may have a move that crosses
from a state no check has touched, and the toll still holds. On Section~\ref{sec:toll}'s board extended with
CHECK, the run $[\mathrm{VOUCH}]$ from NO crosses with one mark, and so does
$[\mathrm{CHECK}, \mathrm{VOUCH}]$. Making a crossing depend on an earlier check is a
condition on the step function, which a particular system can impose and the definition does
not.
\end{remark}

[80] Is there only one such machine? No. One structure, two free choices.
But the billed machine isn't a different \emph{structure}. It's the same record, steps and
halting with a different
price list. So the claim worth keeping is sameness \emph{up to the price schedule}: compare two
machines by their record, their halting and their steps, through their maps down to the
base, and don't compare charges. Fix which event the record is, and the bill stops mattering;
a flat surcharge, or any surcharge that depends on the current state, sits on the same
structure. That's the first free choice. The walk above is the whole argument, and it's
checked over an arbitrary base, with any surcharge that depends on the current state: the
billed machine is base-driven (\code{nec\_w\_billed\_honest}), has the same latch event
(\code{nec\_w\_billed\_same\_latch}), steps along with the original on record, halting and base
(\code{nec\_w\_billed\_same\_up\_to\_schedule}), and pays each step's cost plus the surcharge
(\code{nec\_w\_billed\_step\_cost}), all in NecWArgued.v.

[86] The order, said exactly
So in a partial order the cups are the place, and in the preorder I built they aren't. That second
half holds in every preorder that isn't partial, by one line: if
$x \le y$ and $y \le x$ with $x \ne y$, then a cup that's up at either one is up at the other, by
transitivity. So the cups fix the place if and only if the order is partial
(\code{nec\_f\_same\_rung\_same\_cups}, \code{nec\_f\_non\_partial\_cups\_fail} and
\code{nec\_f\_cups\_iff\_partial}, NecFCups.v). My first guess was right on every
strip I could draw, and it was right because of a rule I hadn't noticed I was leaning on. On a
bare preorder, putting two places on the same rung tells the toll not to care about moves between
them, and it doesn't. I don't think that's a defect. It's the order telling you what you agreed to
count.

\code{ax\_a2\_iff\_views} (AxCore.v, AxLatch.v), with the one-cup floor as \code{two\_point\_nfi}.
When the cups only ever go up, as on the strip and the diamond, a step that turns a cup over is
just a step up (\code{ax\_exit\_is\_strict\_rise}).
