[95] How much gets destroyed, on average
And heat? Part II already said where the line is, and it's the same line here. Landauer's
principle, that crushing $c$ bits' worth of possibilities costs at least $c\,kT\ln 2$ of heat on
average, is a premise from physics, and a contested one (Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}). Nothing in
this section proves anything about heat. What the counting does give is this: under that premise,
on a machine with the halving price, a move's cost priced at $kT \ln 2$ per unit,
times the weight of the piles, is always enough
to cover the minimum the premise would
demand, for every spread and every finite family of landing piles. Checked as
\code{ax\_toll\_covers\_landauer\_minimum} (AxInfinite.v). If the premise falls, that sentence falls
with it, and every theorem above it stands, because none of them used it.

[96] Now point it at the record
The answer is no, under premises. The proof is the two index cards, and I'll give you the
premises because they're the whole content. Suppose there were such a decider, one that says yes
exactly when a program admits a shortcut. Build the
flip: ``if the decider says yes about me, behave like a program that admits no shortcut; if
it says no, behave like one that does.'' If the system has a recursion theorem (a program
can be handed its own code), and if that flip is one of the transformations the system can
carry out, and if ``admits a shortcut'' depends only on behaviour (two programs that behave
the same agree on it), and if there's at least one program each way, then there's a program
that behaves exactly like its own flip, and whatever the decider says about it is wrong.
Both cases close in a line each. That's \code{structural\_shortcut\_undecidable}, which rules out a decider that says yes if and
only if a program admits a shortcut, stated for \emph{any}
substrate with those fields. (A substrate here is the bare shape the class asks for: programs, a run that may fail
to give back a state, a counter with a step relation it never goes down along, an
encoding of programs as data, a notion of ``representable'' transformation, and the recursion
theorem for those. Nothing about what the states are made of. The smallest checked
instance is the nat family below.) It's Rice's trick one floor over, and I should be careful
with that. Rice's theorem is stated for the functions Turing machines compute. This wall is
the same diagonal with different premises: it carries its own recursion theorem and its own
list of representable transformations, and it picks the property. So it isn't a corollary
of Rice as Rice stated it, and it doesn't show that any property is harder or easier to decide
than classical halting. And
here's something about its proof that I want you to have: it never touches the counter,
the toll, or the step relation. It uses the recursion theorem, representability of the flip,
the two witnesses, and extensionality, and nothing else. (The file's header calls the
substrates it covers ones that respect the toll. The
proof uses neither the toll nor the counter's climbing. It's the diagonal, clean.)

[97] Now point it at the record
Checked as \code{structural\_shortcut\_undecidable} (StructuralUndecidability.v; the correctness it
rules out is $d(p)$ true exactly when $A(p)$), stated over the
class \code{Substrate} (Substrate.v) and its extension \code{WithShortcutPredicate}. Every field named
above is a premise; the theorem is about every substrate that supplies them.

[98] Now point it at the record
The \emph{nat family} (\code{nat\_self\_undecidable}): a tiny substrate built so that program two is the
Contrarian for a given candidate decider d, and for every d, d is wrong about program two. It's
a \emph{family}, one instance per d, and its recursion theorem holds by construction. It's the
index cards made formal and nothing more.

[99] Now point it at the record
Checked as \code{nat\_self\_undecidable} (NatSubstrateInstance.v), with the built-in recursion
theorem \code{nat\_recursion\_theorem}; the general form, no decider whose flip is representable in
$N_d$, is \code{nat\_structural\_shortcut\_undecidable} in the same file.

[100] Now point it at the record
And the one where the recursion theorem is \emph{proved} from the reduction rules rather than assumed:
\emph{L}, the weak call-by-value lambda calculus that Yannick Forster and Gert Smolka presented in
2017 as a model of computation in Coq, and which a group at Saarland has since used as the
seed of a whole library of undecidable problems. The development here builds its own copy of
L from the reduction rules, so the L results below assume nothing from that library. There,
\code{L\_recursion\_theorem} is a theorem with a proof, Rice's theorem follows (\code{L\_rice}), halting
follows (\code{L\_halting\_undecidable}), and the diagonal is reproved in the shape of the substrate
theorem (\code{L\_structural\_shortcut\_undecidable}), for any property of programs that depends only
on what they compute and that holds of one program and fails of another. The statement
mentions no shortcut. That's the diagonal for a fixed model. It isn't an instance of the generic class
(the class wants a run map that always answers, with a state or with nothing, and L's evaluation
can run forever without ever answering), so it's the argument \emph{reproved}. The
generic theorem isn't \emph{applied} here. Both are true; they're different sentences.

[101] Now point it at the record
Checked as \code{second\_recursion}, \code{L\_recursion\_theorem}, \code{L\_rice}, and \code{L\_halting\_undecidable}
(LRecursion.v), with the quoting term's specification \code{Q\_spec} and the substrate-shaped form \code{L\_structural\_shortcut\_undecidable} (no decider whose flip L can
compute says true exactly when the property holds) in the same file. The file uses only Coq's standard library.

[102] How to kill it
Three: for L, produce a closed term that decides some extensional property holding of one
closed term and failing of another. That breaks \code{L\_rice}, which rests on the reduction rules
and Coq's standard library and nothing else.

[103] Proof-of-stake finality: the price is on going back
Do it with three validators. Let A and B vote one branch, B and C vote a competing branch.
Each pair is two of three. Both branches finalize. Now read the rule: B cast two different votes
with the same target epoch. B is the third that gets slashed; A and C are clean. That's what a fork costs
and who pays, and the checked work has exactly this instance (\code{casper\_fork\_exists},
\code{casper\_fork\_slashable}), so the theorem's premise isn't vacuous: forks of that kind can be
built, and the theorem fires on them.

[104] Proof-of-stake finality: the price is on going back
The fragment: a transcription of a published Coq proof of Casper's accountable safety
(the casper-proofs development from Runtime Verification and the University of Texas at
Austin, itself based on Yoichi Hirai's proof in Isabelle, another proof checker; proofs redone on the standard library), with validator sets as predicates and
quorum intersection as a premise. A \emph{quorum} is a set of validators big enough to act, here
two thirds of the stake, and \emph{quorum intersection} is the arithmetic of the three-validator
example: two thirds plus two thirds is four thirds, so any two quorums share at least a
third. The fragment takes the overlap as given. It doesn't do that arithmetic. No deposits,
no rewards, no fork choice (the rule for picking a branch to build on), no connection to
anything else in this book. \code{accountable\_safety}: a finalization fork implies that a set of
validators the size of that overlap, at least a third of the stake, is slashed. And
\code{finalization\_without\_slashing}: a one-validator chain finalizes and nobody's slashed, which
is the ``write is free'' sentence in a degenerate setting, and only there.

[105] Proof-of-stake finality: the price is on going back
Checked as \code{accountable\_safety} (CasperFFG.v), a transcription of the casper-proofs
Core/AccountableSafety file at a pinned commit with proofs redone on the standard library;
validator sets are predicates, and both premises are fields of the setting record, so they
are hypotheses of every theorem. The record reading is \code{conflicting\_records\_are\_priced}
(CasperRecordReading.v).

[106] Proof-of-stake finality: the price is on going back
Checked as \code{casper\_fork\_exists} and \code{casper\_fork\_slashable} (CasperForkWitness.v), and
\code{finalization\_without\_slashing} (CasperRecordReading.v).

[107] Gas: the one that charges a fee at the write
Now the leak, and it's Section~\ref{sec:counter}'s leak. Set a slot and then clear it \emph{in the same transaction} and
you get a refund. Count it through: first write twenty-two thousand one hundred; clearing it
costs a hundred and credits a refund of nineteen thousand nine hundred; net, twenty-three
hundred. That's \code{revoked\_write\_nearly\_free}, and \code{persistent\_write\_priced} is the other half:
leave it set and you pay at least twenty thousand. The write is priced.
The \emph{revoked} write nearly isn't. But I have to add what the fragment leaves out, because twenty-three hundred
on its own misleads: the realized refund is capped at one fifth
of the gas the whole transaction used (that's the 2021 change from Section~\ref{sec:counter}, the one that ended
the refund-hoarding token's advantage). So a real set-then-clear pays a lot more than twenty-three
hundred. How much more? The cap is a fifth of everything the transaction used, and that
includes the twenty-one thousand every transaction pays just for existing. Take a
transaction that does nothing but set and clear the slot: it uses 43,200 gas, so the cap is
8,640, so it pays 34,560, which is 13,560 on top of the base. (Look at the slot alone and
forget the base, which the rule doesn't do, and you'd get 17,760.) Either way it's far above
twenty-three hundred. The counter-level net is twenty-three hundred. The bill is not.

[108] Gas: the one that charges a fee at the write
Checked as \code{persistent\_write\_priced} and \code{revoked\_write\_nearly\_free} (EVMStorageGas.v),
transcribed from the execution specification's storage instruction and gas table at a
pinned commit. The end-of-transaction refund cap of one fifth is not in the fragment.

[109] Gas: the one that charges a fee at the write
Then the wrapper, which is a different kind of object, and the walk near the end is about
exactly that difference. It's a \emph{gas schedule} in the abstract: any states, any steps, a cost
per step, a yes/no commitment flag, and a charge predicate saying which steps get billed. It
formalises no EVM (the Ethereum virtual machine) at all. It is Section~\ref{sec:windows}'s result
with the whole-run ceiling, the one that pins the amount as well as which steps pay, under a new
name, and the file says that too. Its two halves are worth reading separately. Leave one
committing step unbilled, and a one-step run commits for nothing
(\code{undercharged\_opcode\_admits\_free\_commitment}), so the floor fails outright; one missed commit
is enough. Bill one step that doesn't commit, and the schedule charges more than the number
of commits on that one-step run (\code{overcharge\_breaks\_exactness}), so the ceiling fails. And
the class isn't empty: in any system, the bill that charges one exactly on a step that raises
the flag and nothing on any other meets both halves (Section~\ref{sec:windows}'s step price).

[110] Gas: the one that charges a fee at the write
Checked as \code{gas\_schedule\_exactness}, which proves both directions,
\code{undercharged\_opcode\_admits\_free\_commitment},
\code{undercharged\_opcode\_breaks\_certification\_floor} and \code{overcharge\_breaks\_exactness}
(GasMetering.v); \code{gas\_schedule\_exactness} is \code{exact\_commitment\_pricing\_characterization}
(CommitmentPredicateAdequacy.v) carried over unchanged.

[111] Attestation: hash chains, and a quote about the past
Checked as \code{permanent\_write\_has\_logical\_payment} (PricingPhysicsAudit.v), from
\code{permanent\_at\_flip\_is\_not\_injective} (PermanentRecordPricing.v); the proof is
Theorem~\ref{thm:perm-flip-merges} with permanence required only of the one instruction $i$.
The second half is \code{permanent\_flip\_merges\_on\_yes\_side} (PermanentRecordPricing.v): if
the step were one-to-one on the states that read on together with $s$, it would map the states
that read on onto themselves, and then $s$ would land where some state that reads on already
lands.

[112] Attestation: hash chains, and a quote about the past
What the quote can do is say what was \emph{measured}.
It can't say the measured code is correct, and it can't say anything about runtime state
that was never folded in. A correctly measured buggy program attests beautifully. The
fragment proves that exact limit: one register, a hash about which nothing is assumed, a quote as a pair, and no
function of the quote decides a runtime flag the quote ignores
(\code{quote\_cannot\_attest\_unmeasured\_state}), which is Section~\ref{sec:windows}'s cube again. And a companion shows
that a sign-and-verify interface \emph{alone} gives no authenticity (a scheme where verify always
says yes passes the interface), so a cryptographic premise has to be added; the fragment
doesn't model signatures, formats, or a freshness check (the nonce is just a field the
verifier fixes).

[113] Attestation: hash chains, and a quote about the past
Checked as \code{quote\_cannot\_attest\_unmeasured\_state} (TPMQuoteGap.v). In the same file,
\code{quote\_decides\_measured\_claims} says any Boolean claim about the retained digest can be read
off the quote. The companion is \code{tpm\_interface\_authenticity\_refuted}
(TPMQuoteAuthenticity.v): a scheme whose verify always says yes satisfies the sign-and-verify
interface while accepting a signature no key produced.

[114] Certificate transparency: visible, unpriced
Do the inclusion proof by hand, because it's the most satisfying ten minutes in this section.
Take the RFC's own seven-leaf example tree and verify that leaf d3 is in it from the path
the log hands you: three hashes and a rule about which side to hash on, depending on
whether your position is odd. Three hashing steps and you land on the root. The checked work runs the
RFC's own examples (\code{rfc9162\_example\_inclusion\_d3} and its siblings, including the awkward
lone-leaf case) through a transcription of the RFC's verifier control flow with a symbolic
hash. Verifier logic only: no signatures, no gossip (auditors comparing
the views they were shown), no misbehaving log.

[115] Certificate transparency: visible, unpriced
Checked as \code{rfc9162\_example\_inclusion\_d3}, its siblings \code{rfc9162\_example\_inclusion\_d0},
\code{rfc9162\_example\_inclusion\_d4} and \code{rfc9162\_example\_inclusion\_d6},
\code{rfc9162\_example\_consistency\_4\_7}, and \code{rfc9162\_inclusion\_boundary\_safe} (RFC9162Merkle.v).
The append-only history is list append, and \code{ct\_extension\_preserves\_entries} says appending
keeps every old entry.

[116] Proof-carrying code: no price, no permanence
Checked as \code{pcc\_checker\_accepts\_iff\_vc}, \code{pcc\_certificate\_implies\_vc}, and
\code{pcc\_unsafe\_program\_rejected} (NeculaPCC.v, definitions in NeculaPCCTarget.v).

[117] One example, three levels
The other four sit on the same three levels, and only one more wrapper is spelled out in this
section. Casper's transcription is the accountable-safety proof above; its wrapper is a small
gadget with the stake at risk handed in. A finalize step that risks nothing turns the record
on for free, which breaks Section~\ref{sec:toll}'s toll (the file calls that free forgery), and one that risks at
least one unit has a floor (\code{nothing\_at\_stake\_is\_free\_forgery}, \code{slashing\_finality\_floor},
PoSFinality.v). It records exposure to loss (no collateral is destroyed in it), has no Casper
checkpoints in it at all, and is priced by stipulation, the same way the gas wrapper is.
Attestation, transparency and proof-carrying code have transcriptions that prove what their
verifiers do and no price theorem, because their specifications carry no price. Their
wrappers, which I don't spell out here, are Section~\ref{sec:windows}'s verifier argument told in each system's vocabulary,
with a colliding pair behind each one, and that argument is all they carry here.

[118] What's checked
Two pointers on the same $R$ agree on every state of $R$, so the pointer is unique with no list
of rivals (\code{pointer\_on\_unique}). And the definition of independence has a consequence I
didn't put in on purpose.

[119] What's checked
Checked as \code{independent\_copies\_everywhere\_are\_constant} (PointerObservable.v). So
separate parts of the world can each carry an exact copy of a real record only on the states the
world actually reaches, where the copying made them agree, and that's why the definition asks for
copies on a set $R$ of reachable states. Zurek's copies are the same: the light carries the
dial's reading because the dial put it there.

[120] What's checked
Checked as \code{ledger\_carriers\_independent}, \code{ledger\_copying\_alone\_does\_not\_separate},
\code{ledger\_finalized\_is\_pointer} and \code{ledger\_gas\_not\_relied\_on} (PointerObservable.v).
It's a sanity check with maps I wrote, and it shows only that the tightened definition can tell
the two apart where plain copying can't.

[121] What's checked
The thesis leans on two notions with no precise mathematical definition: what it is to
``faithfully model'' a system, and which systems count as ``deployed.'' Those two tie the
definitions to the world, the way ``computable in the plain-English sense'' does in the
Church-Turing thesis, and no definition can do that job, so the claim is a thesis. The other
two terms it needs, independent carriers and the events a step rule could meter, are defined in
Definition~\ref{def:u14-reliance}. Its formal shape is checked as a schema,
\code{pointer\_thesis\_schema} (PointerObservable.v), with the class, the reachable states, the
certification event and the priced records as inputs; nothing about them is proved.

[122] What's checked
Checked as \code{toy\_cert\_unique\_pointer} and \code{toy\_work\_not\_proliferating} (PointerObservable.v).

[123] What's checked
A survey of twelve events, one checked theorem: for each of the five systems in Section~\ref{sec:models}, the
designated event (finalized, committed, attested, logged, checked) proliferates in the
observer map I wrote for that system, and a rival (proposer work, a scratch counter,
measurement noise, submission latency, prover steps) doesn't. The five together are one
statement (\code{five\_labeled\_models\_have\_selected\_pointer}), an instance of the schema above.
Plus a symmetric message-authentication code, which does \emph{not} proliferate, and a digital
signature, which does. Twelve rows. And a thirteenth that I think is the most important one: swap which bit
every observer copies, and the criterion picks the \emph{other} event. The criterion selects
whatever the observer map copies. The map is where the argument lives, and I wrote the maps.
So the five ``confirmations'' are five instances of one construction with the fields renamed.
I'm saying that twice on
purpose, so it can't slip past once.

[124] What's checked
Checked as \code{twelve\_candidate\_measurements\_checked} and \code{swapped\_event\_is\_pointer\_checked}
(RecordProliferationSurvey.v, statements in RecordProliferationSurveyTarget.v); the five
designated models together are \code{five\_labeled\_models\_have\_selected\_pointer}
(PointerObservableReductions.v), an instance of the indexed form of the criterion,
\code{pointer\_criterion\_holds\_indexed}.

[125] What's checked
Checked as \code{blind\_observer\_blocks\_proliferation} and \code{labeled\_model\_verdicts}
(PointerObservableCounterexamples.v). The theorem is about the observer maps. That a third
party in deniable authentication really cannot be convinced is the modelling claim, and the
model has no transcript, key or verifier in it to check that against.

[126] What's checked
The second stronger claim: \emph{agreement plus accuracy plus locality imply permanence}. That is,
if all observers agree, each sees the event exactly, and each updates by the same local rule
with no coordinator, then the recorded event can't be undone. Refuted by the smallest thing
imaginable: one bit, two observers who both see it, and the rule ``flip.'' All three conditions
hold and true becomes false in one step (\code{toggle\_game\_refutes\_strong\_pointer\_necessity}). Do
it on paper; it takes a minute and it's humbling. What \emph{does} give permanence is durability,
``an observer's true view stays true,'' added as a premise, and then it follows in a few lines
(\code{durable\_consensus\_implies\_permanence}). So durability is the premise doing the work, and
durability is Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}'s permanence wearing a different coat. Agreement doesn't buy it. Do the
paper version again with one change, the rule ``stay true once true'' in place of ``flip,'' and
the bit can't fall, because the rule won't let it. The observers agreeing has nothing to do with it. (The
machine I build later has a reading that's durable in exactly this sense: its flag never comes
down, by its step rule (Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-toll}).) None of this rules out a narrower theorem with real assumptions about the network,
the adversary or durability; it rules out these two strong forms.

[127] What's checked
Checked as \code{toggle\_game\_refutes\_strong\_pointer\_necessity} and
\code{durable\_consensus\_implies\_permanence} (EcosystemGame.v, definitions in
EcosystemGameTarget.v).

[128] The end of the logic
The obvious question to ask of all that is whether the toll is the only way. A theorem saying so
would be the best thing this book could hold: a short list of requirements that any account of
what a step costs has to meet, whatever field it comes from, with the toll and the counter the
only accounting left standing. I looked for that theorem, and the answer I can give you is no, and
the no is small enough to check on three states. Call them blank, once and twice, with once and
twice reading yes. The flip takes blank to once and leaves the other two where they are. The
smooth takes both once and twice to twice and leaves blank alone. The smooth forgets something, so
the price on merging from Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary} charges it a mark. It raises nothing, so the toll
leaves it free. Now try requirements that name no reading: no charge on a move that changes
nothing, none on a move that forgets nothing, the same bill when you rename the states, a total
that adds up along a run, a bill that looks only at the step, and one mark on the flip from blank. The toll, read as
the bill that charges the flip from blank and nothing else, meets every one of them on this machine.
So does the price on merging. They disagree on the
smooth, so that list doesn't pick between them. What picks the toll is a requirement that names
the record: over any run the total is never more than the number of times the reading went up.
With the floor, that leaves exactly one bill, one mark per raise and nothing else
(Section~\ref{sec:windows}), and it rules the price on merging out, because the smooth costs that price a
mark and raises nothing. All of that is checked on the three states: \code{nec\_f\_three\_toll\_meets},
\code{nec\_f\_three\_merge\_meets}, \code{nec\_f\_three\_bills\_differ},
\code{nec\_f\_three\_ceiling\_picks\_toll} and \code{nec\_f\_three\_merge\_fails\_ceiling}, with the
general uniqueness as \code{nec\_f\_record\_ceiling\_unique} (NecFThreeState.v). The only renaming
of the three states that respects the moves is the identity (\code{nec\_f\_three\_automorphism\_id}),
so the renaming requirement asks nothing there. So the uniqueness is real and it's small. It holds once you've said which
reading you're charging for, and which reading deserves the charge is the pointer thesis,
which is still a thesis.

[129] The machine
The real kinship is older, and it's somewhere else. Picture the table and the counters shared
by many processors, with CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY each done as one indivisible step. Maurice
Herlihy (1991), a computer scientist who has spent his career on how many processors can share
memory safely, ranked shared objects by how many processors each one can bring to agree on a
single value with nobody ever waiting on anybody. Test-and-set, an instruction that writes a
cell and reports what was there, brings exactly two. A sticky bit, the idea of Serge
Plotkin (1989), a Stanford computer scientist working on distributed algorithms, is a cell the
first writer jams for good, with the first writer's value, and everyone after can read it; it
brings any number. I guessed that the shared object would sit at the top with the sticky bit,
because a write that kills every fact about a counter, together with a commitment other
processors can see, looks like a way to build one. Ranking the object takes its operations, what
each one returns, which steps are indivisible, how it starts and which processes run it, and then
a proof that every processor decides, they all decide the same value, the value is one somebody
proposed, and nobody waits. Here it is, with every instruction one indivisible step and the whole
state readable, and the object is at the top. The reason is the order of the fact table. Each
processor CHECKs a property that holds of a counter at zero, PZero to propose 0 and PEven to
propose 1, then reads the table and decides the bit of its oldest fact. The table only gains
entries at the front, and a CHECK that finds it full trips the trap, after which nothing moves, so
the first CHECK of the run fixes the oldest fact for good. Every processor that decides decides
that bit, the bit is somebody's proposal, and each one decides after two of its own steps
whatever the others do (\code{sc\_agreement}, \code{sc\_validity}, \code{sc\_wait\_free},
SmallConsensus.v). The version rule and the commitment play no part. The trap alone doesn't do
it: it records that something trapped, and it doesn't hand everyone the first writer's value.

[130] The machine
Now take the version rule away, let failures change nothing, keep only the properties ``at least
$n$'' so that a checked property only ever turns true, drop the decrement, and make the fact
table, the commitments and the flag sets and latches that only grow. I guessed that then the
object drops to the bottom rung, the level of plain memory, which is the shared-memory face of
what the database researchers Joseph Hellerstein and Peter Alvaro (2020), at Berkeley and Santa
Cruz, call CALM, where monotone means nobody has to coordinate. That's proved too, by Herlihy's
own kind of argument. Let the updates return nothing and let the whole state be readable. If at
every state, of any two updates, one leaves the state alone, or the two commute, or one
overwrites the other, then no protocol brings two processors to agreement with nobody waiting
(\code{mc\_no\_consensus}). The monotone object has that property, with registers beside it,
because an instruction that would succeed still succeeds after anything else has run
(\code{mc\_mono\_interfere}, \code{mc\_mono\_no\_consensus}, MonotoneConsensus.v). The toll
changes neither result. Neither idea is mine: the top is Plotkin's and the bottom is the monotone
fact that field already knows.

[131] The machine
Their setting is small enough to check here whole. A model is any set of processes with a step
relation and a way to compose two processes that doesn't care about order or grouping. Composition
is monotonic when whatever a process can reach alone, it can still reach with anything set beside
it. A consensus process is one where any number of copies, run together, can end up agreeing on
either of two outcomes, and once a run has one outcome it can never show the other. Their theorem
goes through in a few lines: run half the copies to one outcome and the other half to the other,
and if neither half can block the other, the two outcomes meet on the same state
(\code{cs\_no\_consensus\_monotonic}, ConsensusSeparation.v). Then the machine goes into it.
A process is a shared machine state with a crowd of threads, each proposing a bit, and composing
two processes pools their threads on one state. One thread proposing each bit is a consensus
process in exactly their sense, for the outcomes ``some thread decided 0'' and ``some thread
decided 1'' (\code{cs\_machine\_consensus}), so by their theorem that composition isn't
monotonic (\code{cs\_machine\_not\_monotonic}). The small machine sits on their non-monotonic
side, checked. What puts it there in the run is the order of the fact table, the same as above;
a store that refuses a stale commit for good, as the version rule does, refuses in the same
spirit. The shared-memory version of their split is the pair of results above: the small
machine's object reaches agreement for any number of processors, and the monotone variant reaches
it for none (\code{sc\_agreement}, \code{mc\_mono\_no\_consensus}).

[136] It computes everything two counters can

[137] It computes everything two counters can
Is ``given directly'' just a convenience, or does it bite? It bites, and somebody found
exactly where. In 1972 Rich Schroeppel, then at the MIT AI Lab, wrote a ten-page memo titled
``A Two Counter Machine Cannot Calculate $2^N$.'' He spells out the convention first: a counter
machine computing a function starts with $n$ in one counter and zero in the others. Then he gives
a proof that a two-counter machine started that way can't compute
$2^n$. His theorem is checked here, for every program of the small machine
(\code{tc2\_no\_pow}, Tc2Pow.v) and for every machine with a finite control whose counters are
read only through zero and parity above a threshold (\code{am\_no\_power\_of\_two}), by the same
chain of stages that proves the theorem about multiplying near the end of the chapter
(Theorem~\ref{thm:no-plain-recursion}): along an arithmetic progression of inputs the outputs grow
by a fixed amount, and powers of two don't. So the packing Minsky needs is precisely the thing two plain counters can't do for
themselves. Hand the small machine its inputs straight and there are functions of them it
can't compute; hand it the inputs already packed and it computes anything a Turing machine
can. That's no contradiction with the undecidability above, which ranges over every program
and every starting pair, packed ones included. The base hides nothing. It just needs its
input pre-chewed, and the chewing is somebody else's.

same floor is proved directly, without the link, as \code{nfi\_floor} (EarnedCore.v).

[150] The exact price is three

[151] The exact price is three
The three is three independent units, one for each part of a clean start. A flag that is down
buys the first: a run that raises it costs at least one (\code{nfi\_floor}). An empty channel buys
the second: with the flag down and the channel empty, a run that raises the flag costs at least two
(\code{nec\_s\_floor\_two}). An empty table buys the third, and that is the theorem above. Leave out
any one part and its floor fails, and a cheaper certificate is there: with the flag already up it
costs 0, with a commitment already in the channel 1, and with a live fact already in the table 2
(\code{nec\_s\_clean\_conjuncts\_each\_buy\_one}, \code{nec\_s\_min\_cost\_needs\_flag\_down},
\code{nec\_s\_min\_cost\_needs\_empty\_channel} and \code{nec\_s\_min\_cost\_needs\_empty\_table}). The
exact condition is \code{nec\_s\_floor3\_iff}: every run from a state that raises the flag costs at
least three exactly when the flag is down and one of two things holds: the trap is up,
or the channel is empty and
every fact in the table is about an older version of its counter. And three is paid exactly from
every clean start whose trap is down (\code{nec\_s\_witness\_from\_every\_clean\_start}). All of these
are in NecSClean.v, except \code{nfi\_floor}, which is in EarnedCore.v.

[152] The exact price is three
Checked as \code{earned\_run\_check\_can\_fail} and \code{earned\_run\_refused\_forever}
(EarnedCore.v), with the program named \code{earned\_run}, and the lemma
\code{run\_prog\_trapped} for a trapped run standing still. The version whose check can't
fail, CHECK \emph{at least 0} $A$; COMMIT \emph{at least 0} $A$; CERTIFY, certifies from every
start for exactly 3 (\code{min\_cost\_tight}, with that program named \code{witness}). So the
floor of three is reachable from any start at all, and that's the machine declining to police
trivial checks. On that program the check distinguishes no inputs.

[153] The exact price is three
And that's every piece of the model, standing on six instruction kinds: a base that computes
everything two counters can, a ledger that's exact, a toll the universal floor applies to, a
flag that's a latch of the base, a window that can't see the record, and, from a clean start, a commitment that can't
happen unless a check of the same claim,
on the same edition, came first and was right. The formal counterparts are in EarnedCore.v and EarnedCoreLinks.v.

[154] What it takes to be certified
Defined as \code{claim\_language}, \code{lang\_interface}, \code{thiele\_complete\_over},
\code{thiele\_interface}, \code{thiele\_complete\_with} and \code{thiele\_complete}
(ThieleComplete.v), with the four clauses as \code{universal\_base\_clause},
\code{earned\_record\_clause}, \code{exact\_toll\_clause} and \code{non\_vacuity\_clause}; the Coq
predicate \code{weakly\_thiele\_complete} asks only for the toll and a universal base. An interface over a language becomes an interface
of the plain kind by taking the language's claims, meanings and ``unchanged'' as its own
(\code{lang\_ti}), and the four clauses are asked of that.

[155] What it takes to be certified
The same scope goes on the front matter's ``it isn't more computing power.'' The record (the flag
and the ledger) is never read by the step that computes the next core (\code{base\_blind}), so
erasing it changes no run, and it makes nothing faster. The checker is part of the machine under
the record. With an oracle checker that machine decides what the oracle decides, since whether a
CHECK traps is there for anyone to see; with a checker that's a program, it does what programs do.
And a checker that's a program still makes CHECK a test at unit cost.

[156] What it takes to be certified
Checked as \code{check\_ge\_unit\_cost} (ThieleComplete.v).

[157] What it takes to be certified
One word needs pinning before the theorems, using the three names set at the front of the book.
In clause (c), the \emph{exact toll} is instruction-exact pricing: each of the three acts, CHECK
and COMMIT included, costs exactly one, so the ledger counts the acts that make a claim. Back in
Section~\ref{sec:counter}, one of the cited results (\code{a2\_and\_aara\_iff\_exact}) is about
rise-only pricing in its tightest bill, the \emph{exact schedule}: one mark on the step that
raises the flag and nothing on any other step. Measured against the flag alone, the small machine
isn't rise-only, since CHECK and COMMIT raise no flag and cost one each; measured the way (c)
asks, it is instruction-exact. Neither is wrong. They answer different questions: does the ledger
count the acts, or does it charge the flag and only the flag? Simulation-exact accounting, the
third name, comes up with the universal program $U$ below.

[162] What it takes to be certified
Checked as \code{drop\_meets\_b\_c\_d}, \code{drop\_fails\_a}, \code{drop\_not\_thiele\_complete},
\code{drop\_weakly\_thiele\_complete}, \code{free\_meets\_a\_c\_d}, \code{free\_fails\_b},
\code{free\_not\_thiele\_complete}, \code{free\_weakly\_thiele\_complete},

Clause (c) also fixes something it has no business fixing: the size of a mark. Double every price
on the small machine and it leaves the class, because its CHECK now costs two and (c) asks for
one. A definition that a change of currency breaks would be a strange one, so here is (c) with
the unit left open.

[175] Every computably presented Thiele machine, on one host: driven, decided, priced in whole units, carried as a latch
How far the small machine gets without it is proved, and the count is short. Take a run that
never traps and ends with the flag down. No CERTIFY ran in it, since a passing one raises the
flag and a failing one traps. Every CHECK in it passed, since a failing one traps, and each
passing CHECK filled one of the table's sixteen entries, so there were at most sixteen. Every
COMMIT in it committed a claim the table holds, and the table holds at most sixteen, so at most
sixteen COMMITs can be of claims the run hadn't committed before. That's 32, and the only way
past it is a COMMIT of a claim already committed earlier in the run (\code{pf\_bound\_start},
PayFree.v). A claim names its counter's version, and the version changes at every write, so a
repeat needs its counter left alone since the earlier COMMIT. A guest whose every move writes
its counters can't do that, so it runs out of ways to pay after 32 (\code{pf\_no\_repeat\_bound}).
A simulated program that runs longer than that has to bill somewhere, and PAY is where. Whether some other
host without PAY could do the same job is a question I haven't settled, and I don't claim it.

[176] Every computably presented Thiele machine, on one host: driven, decided, priced in whole units, carried as a latch
The definition of certified Thiele-complete sorts every paid move into CHECK, COMMIT or CERTIFY, so PAY
has to be filed under one of them, and it's filed as a CHECK of the empty claim, a claim that
never holds. That's a filing for the interface. It isn't what PAY does on the machine: a CHECK
that fails on this machine traps, and PAY goes on to the next line. With PAY filed that way the
host stays certified Thiele-complete
(\code{pu\_universal\_thiele\_complete}, UniversalPRun.v), and certified Thiele-complete over its own claim
language, in which the empty claim means nothing (\code{pu\_universal\_thiele\_complete\_over},
UniversalPRun.v), and its floor of three for a flag
raised from a clean start
still holds with PAY present (\code{pu\_multi\_certified\_run\_min\_cost}, EarnedMultiPriced.v).

[177] Every computably presented Thiele machine, on one host: driven, decided, priced in whole units, carried as a latch
A fixed checker that runs whatever program it's handed and always answers needs saying how, so
here is the routine. A claim of the second kind carries a number $r$. The checker decodes $r$
as a counter-machine routine: its instructions, where they sit, which register holds the
answer, which register holds the fuel, and how many registers to load. If $r$ doesn't decode to a
routine, the answer is no. Otherwise the checker loads the routine's registers from $v$, reads a
fuel number $f$ out of $v$ too (the fuel register's entry in $v$'s prime factorisation), runs the
routine for at most $f$ steps, and says yes exactly when by then the routine has left its code
with 1 in its answer register. Every input gets an answer within $f$ steps, so the checker is
total, and what it means is that bounded run: yes if and only if the routine halts within the
fuel the value carries, with answer 1 (\code{cg\_ueval\_total} and \code{cg\_ueval\_iff},
CompilerChecker.v, through the host's one property \code{pu\_heval}, UniversalPCodes.v). That's
how it stays clear of the diagonal. A total evaluator $E$ that agreed with every total yes-or-no
program on that program's code alone would let $g(n) = \neg E(n, n)$ contradict itself at its own
code. This checker never evaluates a program on its code alone: the fuel comes with the claim's
value. The guest compiled from $M$ runs $M$'s reading routine itself and puts at least the step
count it took into the fuel register, and at such a value the checker says yes if and only if the
reading says yes (\code{cg\_checker\_exact}).

[180] Every computably presented Thiele machine, on one host: driven, decided, priced in whole units, carried as a latch
And one machine carried all the way, by proof, so the class isn't empty. Its states are whole numbers, its one
move adds one and costs three, its driver always picks that move, and its reading is ``at least
three.'' The four programs are written out from the library's building blocks and proved to compute
those four things. Loaded into $U_P$ from 0, the host never halts, its flag goes up, at the host
time matching move $n$ its ledger is exactly $3n$ and its flag is up exactly when $n \ge 3$, and
the raised flag stands on a host chain whose slot claims the machine's own reading of a state at
least three (\code{pu\_demo\_computably\_presented}, \code{pu\_demo\_never\_halts},
\code{pu\_demo\_flag\_rises}, \code{pu\_demo\_exact} and \code{pu\_demo\_earned},
PresentedDemo.v). That's proved. Nothing executes $U_P$ on that guest.

[181] A simpler host, with the guest step built in
\begin{definition}[The host]\label{def:u34-host}
A \emph{guest} is a program $P$ of this chapter's machine together with a state $g$ of it
(Definition~\ref{def:u34-step}). A \emph{host state} $h$ has six parts: its own core
$\mathrm{core}(h)$, its ledger $\mu(h)$, its own flag $\mathrm{own}(h)$, the guest program
$P(h)$ held as a list of instructions, the guest state $g(h)$, and the \emph{mirror} $m(h)$, a
yes/no copy of the guest's flag. The host has two kinds of move. $\mathrm{OWN}\ i$ runs the
instruction $i$ on the host's own core, costs what this chapter's machine charges for $i$,
and raises $\mathrm{own}$ exactly when that machine's flag would rise. $\mathrm{GSTEP}\ b$
costs $b$. If the host's core is trapped it does nothing else. Otherwise it moves the host to
its next line and, if the guest's next instruction costs at most $b$, runs that instruction
on $g(h)$; in the same move the mirror becomes $m(h) \lor (\lnot\mathrm{cert}(g(h)) \land
\mathrm{cert}(g'))$, where $g'$ is the new guest state. The host's reading is
$\mathrm{own}(h) \lor m(h)$. Its stored program is $U_0 = [\mathrm{GSTEP}\ 1;\ \mathrm{OWN}\
(\text{INC } A);\ \mathrm{OWN}\ (\text{DEC } A\ 1)]$: one guest step, then two moves that
bring the host back to its first line. (The Coq file calls it \code{U}; I write $U_0$ here so
nobody mixes it up with the universal program $U$.)
\end{definition}

[182] A simpler host, with the guest step built in
And one thing about the definition before the theorems, so you know which of them carry
weight. $\mathrm{GSTEP}$ runs the guest's instruction by calling the small machine's own step on
the guest's state: the guest's step is a primitive of the host. So ``the host runs any guest''
(Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-universal}) is true by construction. The host was built to do exactly
that, and the proof is mostly unfolding. The theorems with something in them come after it.
From a loaded host, or any state where the two agree, the mirror always equals the guest's flag (Observation~\ref{thm:u34-mirror}). Nothing raises the
mirror unless the guest itself passes a CERTIFY inside a guest step the host paid for
(Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-no-free-host}). No $\mathrm{OWN}$ move, in any host program at all,
touches the guest's state or the mirror (the second sentence of that same theorem), and no host
move of either kind rewrites the guest's program (\code{hexec\_gprog}, UniversalThiele.v). And
the mirror is tied to the guest's earned chain: after any host run from a loaded
host, if the mirror is up, the guest's own run from its start contains a
passing CHECK, then a COMMIT of the same claim, then a CERTIFY (\code{host\_mirror\_earned}, UniversalThiele.v). So this
host runs any guest by construction and keeps the guest's record by theorem, as a copy of the
guest's flag. It never earns a flag of its own. $U$ does both the long way: it runs the guest
with its own instructions, and it earns its own flag.

[197] Parts II and III, run on this machine
\emph{Weights, and the partition form.} Two more pieces of the same count. First,
suppose the rivals aren't equally many: give each a whole-number weight, a multiplicity, and call
the sum of the weights of a list its \emph{mass}. The count survives with masses in place of
lengths. If the prior's mass is at most $L(T)$ times the posterior's, the rounded index bits of the
two masses differ by at most $\mathrm{depth}(T)$; so for any Thiele machine a run that paid at
least that many charged steps has a bill at least the drop, and for a certified Thiele-complete machine the
representation theorem goes through with masses and the same five conclusions. On such a machine
the ledger rises by the same number from every start, the number of record moves, so the expected
rise over a weighted prior is its mass times the record moves, and the drop in rounded bits times
the prior's mass is at most that expected rise, with no averaging argument. The weights are whole
numbers, cross-multiplied, and no probability enters. Checked as \code{ent2\_weighted\_bits},
\code{ent2\_wreduction\_covers}, \code{ent2\_weighted\_cs}, \code{ent2\_weighted\_representation},
\code{ent2\_rise\_independent}, \code{ent2\_expected\_rise\_exact} and
\code{ent2\_weighted\_expected\_bound} (EntitlementMore2.v). Second, the covering by fibres has a plainer form
that doesn't need the clause that the fibres add up to the prior. Say the rivals are
\emph{partitioned} by the representative observation when every rival looks like some survivor
under it and no observation class, counted inside the prior, has more than $L(T)$ members. The
fibre lengths then add up to at least the prior, because every rival is in the class of some
survivor, so a partition gives the covering, and the count holds under it. The converse is false:
there are coverings that aren't partitions. Checked as \code{ent2\_cover\_sum},
\code{ent2\_partition\_old\_iff}, \code{ent2\_partition\_to\_reduction},
\code{ent2\_partition\_bits} and \code{ent2\_reduction\_not\_partition} (EntitlementMore2.v).

[198] Parts II and III, run on this machine

\code{ent\_small\_instance}, \code{ent\_small\_questions} (EntitlementSmall.v) and
\code{ent2\_observed\_small\_instance} (EntitlementMore2.v), with \code{ent\_earned\_complete\_with}
saying the small machine meets the definition through its own interface. It's a real narrowing.
The cups of Section~\ref{sec:entitlement} aren't sitting under it in a costume, and the posterior is exactly the set of
rivals the run certifies, so here a proof ties the lists to the run. Nobody supplies them.
Read with Theorem~\ref{thm:ent-run-machine}, the run's two passing CHECKs, replayed from the eight
rivals, leave four and then two, the same two, so the posterior is the one the run makes; each
keeps half, so $k = 1$, and the index bits, 2, are at most $1 \cdot 2$ and at most the ledger's
rise of 4 (\code{ent\_small\_run\_narrowing}, EntitlementSmall.v).

[199] Parts II and III, run on this machine
\emph{A search through $n$ bits.} The same shape at a size you can't enumerate, on the machine of
Definition~\ref{def:u34-multi}, the one with a counter
for every number, with the small machine's property language
of Zero, Even and At-least-$n$, read at registers,
in place
of $\mathsf{Slot}$. That machine is certified Thiele-complete too (\code{ent2\_mmachine\_complete}, MultiThiele2.v). Hide $n = k + m$ bits, one in each of registers 0
to $n - 1$. The program asks $k$ questions, one per bit of the first $k$: CHECK ``at least 1'' of
register $i$ for a 1, CHECK ``is 0'' for a 0. Then it COMMITs the first claim and CERTIFYs. The
prior is every $n$-bit value; the posterior is the $2^m$ values that begin with the answers; the
tree is the complete tree of depth $k$.

[200] Parts II and III, run on this machine
Checked as \code{ent2\_search\_certifies\_iff}, \code{ent2\_search\_posterior\_is\_certified},
\code{ent2\_bit\_search}, \code{ent2\_search\_lands}, \code{ent2\_search\_questions\_floor},
\code{ent2\_search\_observed}, \code{ent2\_questions\_cap} and \code{ent2\_no\_search\_past\_16}
(BitSearch2.v, BitSearchMember2.v, BitSearchObserved2.v). The sixteen belongs to the machine. The
program has no say in it: it's the same ceiling as the item ``Sixteen checks per run, ever'' in the list of what the machine
doesn't do, near the end of this chapter, and it's why this search has $k \le 16$ however many bits
are hidden. Unasked bits can be any number. The lists, the
tree and the fibres are supplied here, as in the representation theorem, and tied to the run only
for this member. The covering still prices only the size of the drop. Its reason goes unpriced.

[201] Parts II and III, run on this machine
\emph{The covering can't be dropped.} It's a hypothesis, and one might hope it's a nuisance one
could remove. It isn't, and the small machine shows it. Fix $n \ge 4$, take the $2^n$ starts with $A$
below $2^n$ as the rivals, and run the three-move chain on the claim ``$A \ge 2^n - 1$'': CHECK,
COMMIT, CERTIFY. It certifies exactly from $A = 2^n - 1$, so the posterior is one start, $n$ index
bits are gone, and the ledger rose by 3. So for $n \ge 4$ the claim ``the index-bit drop is at most
the ledger's rise'' is false for this run, and no tree of depth at most 3 covers the narrowing,
whatever the representative observation. One property with a big constant in it carries $n$ bits in
a single yes-or-no question; what the bound prices is a narrowing whose classes are balanced, a
tree with enough leaves. A narrowing of any other shape is outside it. Checked as \code{ent2\_uncovered\_posterior},
\code{ent2\_uncovered\_claim\_false} and \code{ent2\_no\_cheap\_covering} (CoveringNeeded2.v).

[202] Parts II and III, run on this machine
Checked as \code{ent2\_free\_run\_shift}, \code{ent2\_free\_cannot\_decide\_fast},
\code{ent2\_ladder\_decides}, \code{ent2\_machine\_free\_cannot\_decide},
\code{ent2\_machine\_ladder\_decides}, \code{ent2\_ladder\_free}, \code{ent2\_chain\_decides} and
\code{ent2\_time\_tax} (TimeTax2.v), the first three for two-counter configurations and the next
three for the same programs compiled and run on the machine, with the certifying comparison
$3 + 3\lambda < m$ inside \code{ent2\_time\_tax}, (4) as
\code{nec\_e\_time\_tax\_one\_paid\_move}, and the lower bound and the one-move decider together as
\code{nec\_e\_time\_tax\_free\_needed}
(NecESearch.v); the deciding comparison $1 + \lambda < m$ is arithmetic on those. The exchange is sharper than a
comparison of two particular programs: the free side is a lower bound over every program of
INC and DEC, so no choice of program is involved, and the paid side is constant. The limits: the bound is for
programs that make no record move, INC and DEC with falling off the end as stopping. It doesn't
cover every certified Thiele-complete machine, because the definition doesn't forbid a base move that changes a
counter by more than one unit, and such a machine is a different machine, one the bound doesn't
speak for. And the two routes put their answers in different places, the free one in the line
number and the paid one in the flag; this compares two ways to answer one question. It doesn't
compare two certifications.

[203] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{sm\_host\_rice}, with the reduction's two halves as \code{sm\_rice\_prog\_halts} and
\code{sm\_rice\_prog\_diverges} (SmHostRice.v). A property that depends only on the partial function
a program computes, whether it stops and what it leaves in register 0, is extensional, so it's
covered; the files also derive that case again from the recursion theorem below, as
\code{sm\_host\_rice\_fun} (SmKleene.v).

[204] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{sm\_guest\_rice} and \code{sm\_guest\_halting\_clean\_undecidable} (SmGuestRice.v).
That's the clean start only. Every input is harder, because the standard reduction has to keep the
input safe while the halting instance runs, and two counters have nowhere obvious to keep it. The
way through is at the end of the subsection.

[205] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{sm2\_smn\_all} (SmSmnAll.v), an if and only if statement that carries no premise on register 2, and
\code{sm\_host\_universal} (SmKleene.v); the narrower form of (1), with the premise, is still there as
\code{sm\_smn}. The numbering is \code{sm\_hcode}, \code{sm\_hdecode} and \code{sm\_hdecode\_hcode}
(SmCodes.v). Be exact about one
thing: $U$ is not that universal program. $U$ runs two-counter guests, and a two-counter guest
given its input plainly can't run the evaluator. With inputs written as powers of two one can, as
Theorem~\ref{thm:tc-kleene} near the end of this subsection shows. $W$ runs host programs, and it can, because it's a host program on a machine
with registers.

[206] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{sm\_second\_recursion}, \code{sm\_kleene} and \code{sm\_kleene\_codes}
(SmKleene.v). That fixed point is of the \emph{function}: $e$ and $F(e)$ stop on the same inputs and
leave the same number in register 0. Their ledgers, flags and fact tables can differ, because the
marks $e$ pays are the evaluator's. I'd like a fixed point that agrees on the record too, and the next two theorems say how
much of that can be had. The ledger and the flag can be had. Every register can't.

[207] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{sm2\_kleene\_obs} (SmFixedPoint.v), with the five numbers as \code{sm2\_final\_numbers}
and the evaluator's specification as \code{sm2\_ev\_spec} (SmTally.v) and the replay program as
\code{sm2\_VG\_forward} and \code{sm2\_VG\_halt} (SmFixed.v), where the trap latch is up exactly when the fifth number is 1 and the flag exactly when the fourth is positive; \code{sm2\_kleene\_fun} gives back the
version that compares only the function. Two limits, and the second one is a theorem. First, $e$ pays
after it has computed everything. It is not the way $U$ runs a guest, mirroring every move: $e$ pays
the counts, CHECKs, then COMMITs, then the CERTIFY, whatever order $F(e)$ used, so the order of the
record's events isn't preserved, and for a program $F(e)$ that never stops, $e$ never stops and pays
nothing, though $F(e)$ may pay without bound. The relation says nothing about states in the middle of a
run. Second, no fixed point agrees on every register, or on what the facts name.

[208] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{sm2\_no\_exact} and \code{sm2\_reg\_bound} (SmNoExact.v). A variant of $F$ that also
CHECKs register $r$ and commits to it gives $F(e)$ a fact and a commitment about $r$ that $e$ can't
have, so no fixed point agrees on the fact table alone, or on the channel alone, or on the versions of
the registers (\code{nec\_e\_no\_fixed\_point\_facts}, \code{nec\_e\_no\_fixed\_point\_versions},
NecEHost.v). What survives is what Theorem~\ref{thm:host-kleene-record} states. Whether one more fixed
register could be added to that list I haven't settled either way, and I don't claim it.

[209] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{sm\_no\_inside\_decider} and \code{sm\_no\_host\_decider} (SmKleene.v, SmDecider.v).

[210] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{tc\_rice} and \code{tc\_rice\_dichotomy} (TcRice.v).

[211] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Kleene needs more than keeping an input. It needs a program that runs a program. So write a number
$x$ as $2^x$ pebbles and read a result as the exponent of 2, the way Minsky did. With inputs written
that way, there's one two-counter program, call it $U_2$, that, started on $2^x \cdot 3^e$, stops with $2^y$
exactly when the program numbered $e$ gives $y$ on $x$; specialising a program to a number is exact;
and the recursion theorem holds. ($U_2$ is a different program from the universal host program
$U$, which runs on the machine with a register for every number.) Checked as \code{tc\_universal} and \code{tc\_smn} (TcPacked.v).

[212] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{tc\_second\_recursion}, \code{tc\_kleene} and \code{tc\_no\_fine\_kleene} (TcPacked.v,
TcNoFine.v).

[213] The diagonal, on this machine's programs
Checked as \code{tc2\_no\_mult} (Tc2Mult.v), from \code{am\_no\_multiplier} (Tc2Chain.v), which says
the same for any machine with finitely many control states whose counters are read only through zero
and parity above a threshold, and \code{tc2\_plain\_recursion\_false} (Tc2Plain.v), which gives a
carried-out $F$ with no $e$ equivalent to $F(e)$ on plain inputs.

[214] What it doesn't do
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The check price is flat.} CHECK costs 1 whether it's asking ``is this 0'' or ``is this at
least a billion.'' The property language is fixed and each property is one evaluation, so
``pays for its work'' here means that flat price, the same however hard the
question was. A machine that charged by the difficulty of the check would be a different
machine, and I haven't built it.
\item \textbf{DEC on zero isn't a write.} It doesn't change the counter, so it doesn't change the
version. That's the right call for versions (nothing changed), but it means DEC on zero is
a free way to move the line number without leaving any mark. The idle run in the window
section uses exactly that.
\item \textbf{After a trap, a trace still pays.} On programs, a trap stops the run. On arbitrary
traces, instructions after a trap are each charged their cost and none of them changes the
core. I chose to keep charging so that the ledger stays the plain sum of what was issued
(Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-toll} has no exceptions); the price of that choice is that a trapped
machine can still be billed for nothing.
\item \textbf{``Checked'' means what the property language can say.} On this machine that's \emph{is
zero}, \emph{is even} and \emph{is at least $n$}, and a live fact means one of those holds of one counter's
current value. It doesn't mean the program is correct, or that anything follows from the
property, or that the property was worth checking. So can a proved machine of this kind check
``sorted,'' the claim the opening chapter's table runs on? Yes, through an encoding. A copy of
this machine, instruction for instruction, with its property language left open, has provenance,
checker soundness, no forging and the floor of three proved once for every property language that
comes with an exact equality test and a checker proved equal to its meaning. Give it ``the list
this counter encodes is sorted'' as a property and it certifies a sorted list for three and
refuses an unsorted one forever (\code{sorted\_run\_certifies}, \code{sorted\_run\_certifies\_iff}
and \code{sorted\_run\_refused\_forever}, EarnedGeneric.v). The copy is written out again in its own
file, and no theorem derives it from this one; putting the two side by side is how you check
they're the same machine. What makes that a check of
the list is the proof that the checker means sortedness. Without that proof, ``sorted'' would be a number
some unconstrained code wrote into a counter, and the record would be honest about the counter
and silent about the list.
\item \textbf{The certificate is historical.} Between the COMMIT and the CERTIFY, the counter can
change, and the flag still goes up. The flag says a claim was checked and committed about
an edition. It says nothing about whether the claim is still true.
\item \textbf{One latch, and a channel that moves on.} The flag records that an earned chain
happened, once. After that it stays up, and later CERTIFYs change nothing. The channel is the
part that names a fact: with the flag up from a clean start, it names a fact that some earlier
passing COMMIT committed (\code{certified\_channel\_earned} and \code{channel\_names\_last\_commit}, EarnedCore.v). But a later commitment replaces it, so at the end
it names the last earned commitment, which needn't be the one the flag rose on. Two runs from
$\mathrm{start}(0, 0)$ show it, worked from the instruction table. First run: CHECK \emph{is zero}
$A$; CHECK \emph{is zero} $B$; COMMIT \emph{is zero} $A$; CERTIFY; COMMIT \emph{is zero} $B$.
Second run: the same five instructions with the last two swapped, so COMMIT \emph{is zero} $B$
comes before CERTIFY. Both end in the same state: both counters 0 at version 0, both facts in the
table, the channel holding (\emph{zero}, $B$, 0), the trap down, a ledger of five and the flag
up. In the first run the flag rose at step four, on the commitment about $A$. In the second it
rose at step five, on the commitment about $B$. So the flag is an ever-certified latch: it says
an earned chain happened at some step, and the end state doesn't say which claim or which step.
A receipt naming the claim the flag first rose on would need that claim kept where nothing can
overwrite it; a receipt with a time on it would also need the step number kept; and several
certifications that each last would need a record richer than one flag. None of those is built
or proved here. They're extensions. Several claims need a latch each.
\item \textbf{Sixteen checks per run, ever.} Facts never leave and the table holds sixteen, so a run
gets at most sixteen passing CHECKs in its whole life, and the seventeenth traps. A
certificate needs only one, so this costs nothing for the theorems, but it's a hard wall
for any program that wants to keep checking forever, and it's this machine's small version of the
opening chapter's ``you can't keep all of it.'' A CHECK that repeats a claim already in the
table, same property, same counter, same edition, passes again and takes another entry. So the
wall is sixteen passing CHECKs, and sixteen different claims is a different count. Removing
duplicates, evicting old facts or collecting dead ones would each be a different design, and each
would need its own invariants proved.
\item \textbf{The clean start matters.} Provenance, soundness, no forging and the price of three are
all stated from a clean start. Hand the machine a table pre-loaded with facts nobody
checked, and of course none of that follows. The theorems are about what the machine
produces. What you smuggle in at the door is outside them. Spell the door out once: the theorems
cover any list of instructions anybody can issue, from a clean start, on a step that runs as
written, with nothing else writing to the machine. They say nothing about whoever sets or
restores the start, about a reset, about a device that misbehaves, or about whoever carries the
flag's value to you.
\item \textbf{The universal programs have stated guests.} $U$ runs every one of this machine's
programs from every clean start $\mathrm{start}(x, y)$, with halting, answers, flag and ledger matched (the flag over the whole run, the ledger at matching points). $U_P$ runs every
computably presented Thiele machine, with its reading decided by a program, its record carried as
a latch, and its ledger matched to within a surcharge of at most two when its reading starts at
no, and of three when it starts at yes. When the guest certifies with a ledger below three, no program on
that host, run from a clean start, matches its ledger exactly. A
machine outside that class, with a reading no program decides, isn't covered by either. The
simpler host with the built-in guest step pays one per guest step whatever the step was, so its
ledger is at least the guest's and usually more; $U$'s is exact, and $U_P$'s is exact up to the
surcharge.
\item \textbf{Slow, and fed directly.} Universality is Minsky's, with the inputs placed straight in
the counters. Getting from an arbitrary Turing machine to two counters goes through his
exponential packing, which my files don't redo; the compiler behind $U_P$ uses the library's
version of it, and nothing about it is fast.
\item \textbf{The step doesn't police trivial checks.} CHECK \emph{at least 0} passes on every
value, so CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY on it raises the flag for three from any start (the note after
Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-tight}). A check that can't fail establishes nothing, and the machine raises
the flag for it anyway. The step checks order, aboutness and an honest checker, and it leaves
``was that worth asking'' to whoever reads the record.
\item \textbf{The chairs don't reach it, and the toll is forced another way.}
Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}'s chairs argument forces a toll on a finite machine that pays
for every merge. This machine is neither. Its
counters have no ceiling, so it's infinite. And its free instructions merge states: take a core
at line $\ell$ with $c$ at 1 and version $w$, and a core at line $j - 1$ with $c$ at 0 and
version $w + 1$, everything else alike, and DEC $c$ $j$ sends both to line $j$ with $c$ at 0
and version $w + 1$, for nothing. So full merge pricing fails here, and clause (c) needs those
free moves. What holds is merge pricing asked of the record layer alone: a move that sends two
different states with the same line and counters to one state costs at least one. This machine's
own prices meet it, CERTIFY's merge is a merge of that kind, and so every price list that meets it
charges the flag-raising step at least one, with no finiteness premise
(Theorem~\ref{thm:record-merge-toll}). That premise is Landauer's, named and assumed. And the
exact price of one, where the premise asks for at least one, is still a design choice.
\end{itemize}

[215] What this chapter adds up to
So Part IV has one machine, and it isn't the model. It's a Thiele machine in the front matter's
sense, an instance of the Thiele machine with the toll proved: \code{earned\_cs}
(Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-instance}). It's certified Thiele-complete, in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:u34-thiele-complete}, the sense no clock meets
(Observation~\ref{thm:u34-clock} and Theorem~\ref{thm:u34-tc-instances}): it computes whatever a Turing
machine can, under Minsky's $2^n$ encoding, done outside the machine, its computing is free and can't touch the record, and, from a clean start, its record is earned. And the
universal Thiele machine runs on a host made of its own kinds of instruction: one fixed program,
$U$, with no guest step built in, that runs every one of its programs with halting, flag and
ledger matched exactly and earns its own flag (Section~\ref{sec:universal-host}); and the same
program with one paid move added, $U_P$, that runs every computably presented Thiele machine,
carrying its reading as a latch, its prices to within two when its reading starts at no, and a flag earned on the guest's own
reading (Section~\ref{sec:presented-universal}).

(LiftCore.v, LiftConverse.v).
