[228] Taking the coat off
Two things about that build are worth knowing before you believe a word of the rest. First, the functions
supplied to extraction are the ones the theorems describe. Coq won't extract some of the files directly (a
technical limit on files that rename modules), so the extracted functions are copies with plain
names, and each copy is proved equal to the original, by computation or by a short proof, inside Coq.
The two universal programs are printed by Coq as literal lists and proved equal to the programs in
the theorems. Checked as \code{rlz\_host\_program\_is}, \code{rlz\_phost\_program\_is} and
\code{rlz\_host\_run\_prog\_is} (Realize.v and RealizePriced.v).

[229] $U$ on a real guest
The first row's count is pinned exactly: if the build changed so that it moved by one step, the
comparison would fail. The times are from one ordinary computer and are only there to give you the
scale. That's about twelve host steps for every unit of the guest's code, for every guest step, which
is exponential in the size of the guest. A guest with one CERTIFY in it would have code $2^{32}$, about
fifty billion host steps, and at the measured rate of about 650,000 steps a second it would take
something like twenty-two hours, nearly a day. The guest I wanted, with the whole
chain, has a code near $2^{100}$, which at the same twelve steps a unit is more than $10^{31}$
host steps a guest step; at the measured rate, with this encoding on that computer, that's
around $10^{18}$ years. So $U$ on a guest that
certifies is proved. It isn't run. I'm not going to dress that up. Runs this long only fit in memory
at all because the host's register store can be squeezed during the run without changing any state,
which is a theorem too. Checked as \code{rlz\_sched\_sound} (RealizeCompact.v).

[230] From source programs to the host
Checked as \code{cmp\_final} (CmpFinal.v), with the runner as \code{cmp\_exec\_machine}, \code{cmp\_exec\_sound},
\code{cmp\_exec\_complete} and \code{cmp\_exec\_unhalted} (CmpRun.v) and the source language's own
interpreter as \code{cmp\_interp\_sound} and \code{cmp\_interp\_complete} (CmpLang.v). The
statement says nothing about how many steps the compiled program takes. It's about answers.

[231] The game
The ceiling is easy to reach and costs nothing to write down. Tally one round per question
pair with both players answering zero every time: after the recoding every answer is plus one,
so all four correlators are one. That's the plan that answers zero to everything,
which matches on every pair and so wins the
three pairs that ask for a match, $S = 1 + 1 + 1 - 1 = 2$. On the small machine's host a tally like
that is a few INCs on counters, and INC is free. Nothing in it says the rounds were played; anybody can
tally anything for nothing. The other side, that no finite mixture of plans beats two, is
Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-fine} in Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation}, one direction of Fine's result. The
full equivalence, in the form that speaks of the four averages, is checked too, as
\code{nec\_e\_fine\_iff}; the last part of this section states it.

[232] Where it came from, and why anyone cared
What quantum play does: if the players share an entangled pair and choose what to measure
from their question, the best they can do is S equals two times the square root of two,
about 2.83, a win rate of about eighty-five percent. Boris Tsirelson, a Soviet mathematician who later taught at Tel Aviv University, in a 1980 paper called ``Quantum generalizations of Bell's inequality,'' proved that that's
a \emph{ceiling}: quantum correlations violate Bell, but only up to there. That ceiling, and the
state that reaches it, are checked for strategies in finite dimension, with real amplitudes
(\code{qs\_tsirelson} and \code{qs\_tsirelson\_reached}, QuantumStrategies.v) and with complex
ones (\code{qc\_tsirelson} and \code{qc\_tsirelson\_reached}, QuantumStrategiesComplex.v); and
the ceiling holds in any dimension at all, infinitely many levels included
(\code{ta\_tsirelson}, TsirelsonAlgebraic.v; the next part says how). What's checked
below, besides, is the algebra.

[233] Five sentences that are easy to say by accident
``Faster than light.'' Nothing travels. Each room alone sees fair coins; the correlation shows
up only when you lay the two columns side by side afterward. ``Quantum means random.'' The
average S is fixed by the state; what's random is each single answer; the plans are the less
random side. ``A Bell violation proves nonlocality.'' It rules out local realism, both
assumptions together, and every real test has loopholes with names. ``A tally above two
proves nonlocality.'' No: S is a \emph{population} average, and one finite tally can land above two
by luck. Four rounds, one per question pair, with coin-flip answers, reach S equals four one
time in sixteen. That gap between a population bound and a finite sample is the problem
Yanbao Zhang, Scott Glancy and Emanuel Knill, at NIST in Boulder, work through for real Bell tests (Physical Review A, 2011 and 2013),
and the book doesn't solve it. The way they solve it is worth knowing, because it has the shape
of this book's record. Turn each round into a small bet that no local plan can expect to win,
even a plan that remembers every earlier round, multiply the bets as the rounds come in, and
the French mathematician Jean Ville's inequality, from his 1939 thesis, one of the works that
started the theory of martingales, says the chance the product ever climbs past one over $\delta$
is at most $\delta$. So a flag can go up for good the moment the product crosses, with its error
stated up front. It needs one thing the small machine doesn't have: settings drawn underneath the
program, where the program can't choose them. And how much can one round tell you? Wim van Dam, Richard Gill, a mathematical statistician who later held
the chair in that subject at Leiden, and Peter Gr\"unwald (2005) measured exactly that, in bits of evidence against every local plan. Of the
two-party proofs they compare, CHSH comes out the strongest, ``much stronger'' than the others in
their words, at about 0.046 of a bit per round, a few hundredths, so a convincing record takes
hundreds of rounds. Four won't do. The checked work proves only the narrow thing, that a tally above two is not
explained by \emph{one fixed plan replayed every round}
(\code{chsh\_violation\_rules\_out\_locally\_factorizable\_coupling}), and that narrow thing is what
the sentence has to stay. And ``the PSD test proves the data is
quantum.'' (PSD is short for positive semidefinite, the property of the five-by-five table from
the short version; the next part builds both.) It doesn't, and the direction matters. Passing
implies the five-by-five table, pinned in a way the next part explains, is PSD, and that
implies S squared is at most eight; both are proved. The
correlators of a PSD table like that are the correlators of some quantum strategy. That's
Tsirelson's representation theorem, and it's proved here: the strategy is built on two systems of eight
levels each (\code{tr\_representation}, TsirelsonRepresentation.v). The exact edge of the quantum set
for this game was found by Lawrence Landau, whose 1969 Berkeley thesis was on the quantum theory of local observables, and Lluís Masanes, then at the University of Barcelona (next part), and that's proved here too (\code{am\_arcsine}, ArcsineBoundary.v). Failing says nothing about whether the play was quantum: the check is complete for
the pinned matrix, the quantum set is wider than that, and quantum behaviour can fail it. Every one
of the sixteen plans fails it (\code{nec\_e\_check\_refuses\_all\_plans}), and I'll show you why. What it guarantees is in one paragraph, once
the check is on the host machine.

[234] The five-by-five, and what positive semidefinite means
Picture what quantum play can reach as a field, and every test as a fence. In 2007 Miguel Navascués,
Stefano Pironio and Antonio Acín, in a paper called ``Bounding the Set of Quantum Correlations'' (NPA, after their initials),
came at the edge from outside, resting on necessary conditions. In their terms a table like this one
is the first rung of a ladder: bigger tables of expected products, each PSD, each a necessary
condition for quantum behaviour when its entries are left free, each rung tighter, and (their 2008
paper) converging to the quantum set (strictly, the set from commuting operators, which can differ
from the tensor-product set in general scenarios). Each rung is a fence with the whole field inside
it. The check's table is the first rung with the marginals and the two within-party entries pinned
to zero. Pinning is a stronger demand than the rung makes, so passing puts you inside the first
rung, and a quantum point can still fail. My fence cuts across the field. Lawrence Landau in 1988
and Lluís Masanes in 2003 came at the edge itself, for this two-questions-each case only. I'd have
drawn a flat wall at 2.83. The exact boundary they found is a curved surface (the arc-sine form)
touching 2.83 at one point. It's checked here: a table has a PSD completion exactly when its
entries lie between minus one and one and their arcsines pass four inequalities
(\code{am\_arcsine}), so those are exactly the quantum tables (\code{am\_quantum\_arcsine}, ArcsineBoundary.v).

[235] The check, in integers
The theorem chain, in words: a passed check gives PSD of the five-by-five built from the
counts; contraction and PSD are equivalent, both directions; and PSD forces S squared at most
eight. The names are in the statements below. The underlying algebra is one identity: four times
the sum of the four correlators squared, minus S squared, is a sum of six squares
(\code{chsh\_gap\_is\_sum\_of\_squares}), so if the row sums of squares are at most one, S squared is
at most eight (\code{tsirelson\_squared}), and the point one over root two reaches it
(\code{tsirelson\_tight}). That's Tsirelson's bound as \emph{algebra}, with no Hilbert space (the
mathematical home of quantum states) in it.

[236] The check, in integers
Checked as \code{column\_contractive\_check\_witness\_sound} with \code{npa\_psd\_iff\_column\_contractive}
(Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-check-sound} and Corollary~\ref{cor:chsh-iff} in the appendix).

[237] The check, in integers
Checked as \code{npa\_psd\_implies\_tsirelson\_bound}, with the row
bounds \code{npa\_psd\_zero\_marginal\_implies\_row\_bounds} (CHSHColumnCheck.v), and the identity
\code{chsh\_gap\_is\_sum\_of\_squares} (TsirelsonFromAlgebra.v); this is
Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-psd-tsirelson} in the appendix.

[238] The check, in integers
Checked as \code{nec\_e\_check\_strict}, with \code{nec\_e\_check\_never\_tsirelson} for
$|S| < 2\sqrt 2$, \code{nec\_e\_no\_sqrt8} for the fractions, and \code{nec\_e\_check\_sharp} for
coming as close as you like (NecEChsh.v).

[239] The check, in integers
\begin{remark}[Sound, and incomplete]\label{rem:u23-sound-not-complete}
The pinned matrix is stricter than the quantum question it is usually read against. PSD of
$M(E)$ gives the correlators a PSD completion, since the zeros are one choice of the two free
entries (\code{zero\_marginal\_implies\_elliptope}, ElliptopeCompletion.v). The converse fails.
The all-ones plan has a PSD completion (\code{deterministic\_strategy\_elliptope}), and every
deterministic plan is a quantum strategy (\code{qs\_deterministic\_plan}, QuantumStrategies.v); yet its
minor on rows $A_0, B_0, B_1$ is $1 - 1 - 1 = -1$, so $M(E)$ is not PSD there, and a tally of
one matching round per pair gives $A = 1 - 1 - 1 = -1$ in the integer check, so the check
fails. The second failure is a named theorem on the machine: the tally of one matching round
per pair is refused forever (Proposition~\ref{prop:chsh-demos}). Every deterministic plan fails
the same way. The tally of one round per pair fails the check for each of the sixteen plans
(\code{nec\_e\_check\_refuses\_all\_plans}), so does a plan replayed over any number of rounds once
pairs 00 and 10 have been sampled (\code{nec\_e\_check\_refuses\_deterministic}, NecEChshInt.v), and
no deterministic plan's pinned matrix is PSD (\code{nec\_e\_deterministic\_not\_pinned\_psd},
NecEChsh.v). So a pass proves the bound, and a failure proves only that the tally's pinned matrix isn't
PSD or a pair went unsampled; it says nothing about whether the play was classical or quantum. The free tally from the start of this section is
exactly such a tally: it reaches $S = 2$ and the check throws it out.
\end{remark}

[240] The check, earned on the host machine
Checked as \code{small\_chsh\_check\_iff}, \code{small\_chsh\_clear\_denominators},
\code{small\_chsh\_psd\_iff\_contractive}, \code{small\_chsh\_psd\_tsirelson},
\code{small\_chsh\_check\_tsirelson}, \code{small\_chsh\_eval\_iff} and
\code{small\_chsh\_tally\_of\_code} (SmallChshCheck.v, SmallChshMachine.v). The non-strict bounds of
part (4) are a single checked theorem, \code{small\_chsh\_check\_tsirelson}, with both bounds in one
statement. The strict form of part (4), and the closeness, are checked as
\code{nec\_e\_check\_strict}, \code{nec\_e\_check\_never\_tsirelson} and
\code{nec\_e\_check\_sharp} (NecEChsh.v).

[241] The check, earned on the host machine
Checked as \code{small\_chsh\_flag\_implies\_tsirelson}, \code{small\_chsh\_program\_flag\_implies\_tsirelson},
\code{small\_chsh\_chain\_certifies\_iff}, \code{small\_chsh\_tally\_certifies\_iff},
\code{small\_chsh\_tally\_certifies} and \code{small\_chsh\_tally\_refused\_forever}
(SmallChshMachine.v); the first is printed in Section~\ref{sec:coq-statements}. The strict bound on
the passing tally's score is \code{nec\_e\_check\_strict}, and \code{nec\_e\_check\_sharp} gives
passing tallies as close to 8 as you like (NecEChsh.v). That's the one thing the
machine does with the check, as the section's opening said: a raised flag can stand on it. Five
tallies, each a theorem with its counts written out, show the check isn't vacuous and where its
edges are.

[242] The check, earned on the host machine
Checked as \code{small\_chsh\_demo\_12\_5\_certifies}, \code{small\_chsh\_demo\_14\_5\_certifies},
\code{small\_chsh\_demo\_16\_5\_refused\_forever}, \code{small\_chsh\_demo\_pr\_box\_refused\_forever}
and \code{small\_chsh\_demo\_all\_ones\_refused\_forever} (SmallChshMachine.v). The last one is the
check being stricter than the bound, as a named theorem: a perfectly legal classical play with
$S = 2$ is turned away.

[243] The check, earned on the host machine
So here, in one paragraph, is what the check guarantees. If it passes, then every question
pair was sampled, the four empirical ratios satisfy three polynomial inequalities, hence the
fixed five-by-five is PSD, hence S squared is at most eight. It does \emph{not} guarantee that
the tally came from honest play (a program can INC
whatever counts it likes
for nothing, including four rounds that put S at four). It does not guarantee any
marginal is zero (no marginals are recorded and none are tested). It does not put the players'
state behind the counts. What it does give is a quantum strategy with the same correlators: a
passing tally's correlators have a PSD completion (\code{zero\_marginal\_implies\_elliptope}), and
every point with one is the correlator table of a strategy on two eight-level systems
(\code{tr\_representation}).
And it fails the tally of every single plan, honest classical play included. It has no margin
for sampling noise either. Honest play at Tsirelson's point aims at correlators of plus or minus
one over root two, and there every pair of squared correlators the check adds up comes to exactly
one, the edge of what it accepts. So an honest tally aimed at that point sits right on the line,
and ordinary luck over a finite number of rounds pushes it over most of the time. I simulated it,
drawing each round's answers at Tsirelson's correlators: the check fails 92 percent of the time at
10 rounds per question pair, 87 percent at 100, and about 85.5 percent from 1,000 to 100,000. Fair
coins pass every time from 100 rounds per pair on, and fail about 2.5 percent of the time at 10.
The limit has a closed form. To first order the largest singular value of the noisy correlator
matrix is one plus the larger eigenvalue of a random symmetric two-by-two with independent
Gaussian entries, and the check passes only when that eigenvalue is at most zero, which happens
with probability $(2 - \sqrt 2)/4$. So honest play at Tsirelson's point fails with probability
$(2 + \sqrt 2)/4 \approx 0.854$ in the long run. That's simulation and arithmetic. No theorem in the
files says it.

[244] The check, earned on the host machine
So the files also have the check with a tolerance. For $\varepsilon = p/q$ it tests the same seven
facts in integers with the 1 in the contraction inequalities replaced by $(1 + \varepsilon)^2$. At
$\varepsilon = 0$ it is the check above. A pass means the two-by-two correlator matrix has
operator norm at most $1 + \varepsilon$, and $S^2 \le 8(1 + \varepsilon)^2$. A tally of 100 rounds
per pair with correlators $0.72, 0.70, 0.72, -0.72$ fails the exact check and passes at
$\varepsilon = 1/10$. Checked as \code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_zero}, \code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_sound},
\code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_norm}, \code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_bound} and
\code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_accepts\_noisy\_tsirelson} (NecEChsh.v). In the same simulation, with
$\varepsilon = 3/\sqrt n$ for $n$ rounds per pair, honest Tsirelson play fails it about five times
in a hundred thousand. The price is in the bound: a pass caps $S$ at $2\sqrt 2\,(1 + \varepsilon)$,
a little outside Tsirelson's number. The exact check remains the default. With $p$ and $q$
fixed for a machine instance, the tolerance check also runs as CHECK on the same versioned
registers: its CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY chain certifies exactly when the integer tolerance
test passes, pays three marks on success, and traps permanently on failure. From a clean
start, every raised flag has that earned chain behind it, with the same register value at
CHECK and COMMIT, and establishes the relaxed bound above. Checked as
\code{small\_chsh\_tol\_chain\_iff}, \code{small\_chsh\_tol\_chain\_pays\_three},
\code{small\_chsh\_tol\_refused\_forever} and \code{small\_chsh\_tol\_flag\_bound}
(SmallChshTolerance.v). The Python host in \texttt{thiele\_small/chsh.py} implements the same
integer check and instruction chain. The
check certifies a \emph{shape} of the tally. What the shape means is a question for whoever
supplied the rounds.

[245] A rational stand-in for an irrational number
One number you'll see in the files: 5657 over 2000, which is 2.8285, and two root two is
2.82842... It's the four-decimal round-up, chosen so that rational code can test ``above the
Tsirelson bound'' without square roots, and it's safe because 5657 squared is 32,001,649 and
eight times 2000 squared is 32,000,000, so the fraction sits above root eight by about seven
hundred-thousandths (\code{rational\_tsirelson\_bound}). It's used in a few rational
theorems. It is \emph{not} used in the integer check, and the real-number theorems use root
eight.

[246] Two more tests, as mathematics
The second rung: a nine-by-nine table on I, the four questions, and the four Alice-Bob
\emph{products}, with the correlators and five extra free numbers for three- and four-body
entries. Its two four-body entries are tied, one the negative of the other. That tie is a
constraint, $\langle \{A_0, A_1\} \otimes \{B_0, B_1\} \rangle = 0$: the zero entries of the first
rung don't imply it, and a classical strategy breaks it (\code{q1ab\_tie\_is\_a\_constraint},
QuantumPartitionPSD\_\allowbreak{}1AB.v). So the second rung is level 1+AB on that slice, and
that's what I mean by level 1+AB everywhere in this book. Its PSD is again equivalent to a contraction-like condition (\code{q1ab\_psd\_iff\_column\_contractive}).
The check for it proves something stronger, that the matrix is positive definite, by a cascade of
two Schur steps down to a four-by-four that Sylvester's criterion closes.
Sylvester's name on a test about matrices fits in a way I enjoy, because the word
\emph{matrix} is his. In 1850, in the \emph{Philosophical Magazine}, the English
mathematician James Joseph Sylvester used it for an ``oblong arrangement of
terms'' out of which you cut square blocks and take their determinants, and \emph{matrix} was
already an old word for the place something grows from. That's exactly what the test does:
it cuts the top-left square blocks out of the big grid, one row deep, two, three, four (the
leading minors), and asks each one's determinant to be positive. Was he the first to state the
test? The name doesn't settle that; names stick to people for all kinds of reasons, and I'd
treat this one as a lead.
And a fact that's easy to list without noticing what it means: with all five extra numbers at
zero, the condition certifies a strict \emph{subset of the classical set} (the ball where the four
correlators' squares sum to at most one), forcing S at most two
(\code{q1ab\_check\_at\_gzero\_implies\_classical\_bound}); it can't certify anything above the
classical line. To get a point above two you have to supply the extras
(\code{q1ab\_certifies\_a\_superclassical\_chsh\_point}, S equals 2.4 with one extra at minus one
half). Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation} has the algebra.

[247] Two more tests, as mathematics
Checked as \code{column\_contractive\_iff\_general\_realizable} (GenRealizability.v) and
\code{column\_contractive\_q1ab\_iff\_general\_realizable}.

[248] Two more tests, as mathematics
The third thing: the \emph{elliptope}, the set of correlators for which \emph{some} choice of the two
missing entries (A0 with A1, B0 with B1) makes the five-by-five PSD. Every one of the
sixteen plans is inside it (\code{deterministic\_strategy\_elliptope}), mixtures stay inside
(\code{elliptope\_convex}), every point inside obeys $S^2 \le 8$ (\code{elliptope\_tsirelson}), Tsirelson's point is inside and reaches it (\code{nec\_e\_pinned\_tsirelson\_tight}, NecEChsh.v), the PR box is out
(\code{pr\_box\_not\_elliptope}), and it's strictly bigger than the classical set
(\code{beyond\_classical\_elliptope}). There's a decider for it with a soundness proof, in two
branches: a strict Sylvester check with the completion supplied, and a rational
factorisation certificate of the completed matrix, checked in integers. What's proved is
that a pass puts the correlators inside the set and that no choice of inputs gets the PR box
through (\code{elliptope\_check\_full\_sound}, \code{elliptope\_full\_gate\_never\_accepts\_pr\_box}), and, by
computation, that some named points pass, the all-ones plan and a point that the arc-sine
boundary puts exactly on the quantum edge (\code{am\_pythagorean\_boundary}) among them (\code{gate\_accepts\_all\_ones},
\code{gate\_accepts\_pythagorean\_boundary}).
The file's header also argues that every point with a rational PSD completion has such a
certificate; that is a comment, and no theorem states it. It's a decider with a soundness proof, as
mathematics; none of the small machine's properties is the elliptope check.

[249] The quantum set, checked
Quantum tables are inside the completion set. A strategy's correlator is the inner product of two
unit vectors: Alice's observable applied to the shared state, and Bob's applied to it
(\code{qs\_corr\_dot}). Four unit vectors always have a PSD table of inner products, so every
quantum table has a completion, and Tsirelson's bound follows from that: $S^2 \le 8$ for every
strategy (\code{qs\_tsirelson}, QuantumStrategies.v), and its level-one moment matrix passes the
PSD test with the marginals in (\code{qs\_npa\_psd}). Quantum mechanics uses complex amplitudes,
and the complex case goes through by a plain trick: a complex vector of length $n$ is a real vector
of length $2n$, its real parts followed by its imaginary parts, and the real part of a complex inner
product is the ordinary inner product of those longer real vectors. So the real argument runs
unchanged (\code{qc\_tsirelson} and \code{qc\_npa\_psd}, QuantumStrategiesComplex.v). The two-qubit
strategy from the start of this part reaches $2\sqrt 2$ in both readings
(\code{qs\_tsirelson\_reached}, \code{qc\_tsirelson\_reached}).

[250] The quantum set, checked
The completion set is inside the quantum tables. This is Tsirelson's representation, and the
construction is his. Take a table with a PSD completion. A PSD matrix is a table of inner
products of vectors, always: peel off one row at a time, and a zero on the diagonal forces its
whole row to zero (\code{tr\_gram}). So the completion hands over four unit vectors in four
dimensions, $a_0, a_1$ for Alice and $b_0, b_1$ for Bob, with $a_x \cdot b_y$ equal to the table's
entries. Now the trick. There are four real eight-by-eight matrices, built from the two-by-two ones that swap
two entries or flip the sign of one, that each square to the identity and anticommute in pairs:
multiply any two of them in either order and the answers differ only in sign
(\code{tr\_gamma\_clifford}, checked by computation). Use a vector's four components to mix
those four matrices, and you get an observable that squares to the identity, a legitimate
measurement. Give Alice and Bob a maximally entangled pair of eight-level systems, and the
correlator of two such observables comes out as exactly the inner product of their vectors
(\code{tr\_corr\_inner}). So the strategy built from the four vectors produces the table
(\code{tr\_representation} for real amplitudes and \code{tr\_representation\_complex} for complex,
TsirelsonRepresentation.v). Eight levels on each side is always enough.

[251] The quantum set, checked
The curved edge is the same set again. Each correlator is the cosine of an angle, the angle
between one of Alice's vectors and one of Bob's. Walk the loop $a_0, b_0, a_1, b_1$ and back to
$a_0$: the angle across any one leg can't exceed the sum of the other three, because angles on a
sphere obey the triangle inequality (\code{am\_sphere\_triangle}, ArcsineBoundary.v). Flip one
vector to its opposite and you get the other four inequalities, with $2\pi$ in them. Written in
arcsines, those eight inequalities are Landau and Masanes's four. Going the other way, any four
angles that pass them can be met by actual vectors: put Bob's two at the right angle to each other,
build each of Alice's from the two angles it has to make with them, and check that the determinant that says such a triangle exists comes
out nonnegative. So a table has a PSD completion exactly when its arcsines pass the four
inequalities (\code{am\_arcsine}), and those are exactly the quantum tables
(\code{am\_quantum\_arcsine}). The point $(3/5, 4/5, 4/5, -3/5)$ sits on the edge, because
$\arcsin\tfrac35 + \arcsin\tfrac45 = \tfrac{\pi}{2}$ by the 3-4-5 triangle
(\code{am\_pythagorean\_boundary}), and so does Tsirelson's point (\code{am\_tsirelson\_boundary}).

[252] What isn't proved
One step on paper, and then nothing about dimension. Quantum mechanics also allows systems with
infinitely many levels, and the checks don't need finitely many. Whatever the dimension, a shared
state $v$ gives an expectation on the operators the players measure with:
$x \mapsto \mathrm{Re}\,\langle v, x\,v\rangle$. That one line is the step on paper. The
proofs use only what that expectation always has: you can add, multiply, scale and take
adjoints, the product spreads over sums, and the expectation of $x^*x$ is never negative. Each
measurement is its own adjoint with the expectation of its square at most one, which covers
unsharp measurements too, and Alice's commute with Bob's. That last demand is weaker than putting
them on two separate systems, so the result covers the wider, commuting model as well. On that
footing Tsirelson's sum of squares goes through, and the CHSH value is at most $2\sqrt2$
(\code{ta\_tsirelson}). The same nonnegative form, with each diagonal entry raised to one,
completes the correlator table to a PSD matrix (\code{ta\_realizable}), so the four arcsine
inequalities hold (\code{ta\_arcsine}), and with a unit the full first-level moment matrix,
marginals and all, is PSD (\code{ta\_npa\_psd}, all in TsirelsonAlgebraic.v). The other
direction needs no help from infinity: every point inside the arcsine edge is reached on two
eight-level systems.

[253] What isn't proved
Also: the finite-mixture theorem (\code{fine\_theorem} in the source)
proves only one direction, that a finite local model satisfies the bound, and its converse as
stated is false: a table with $S = 0$ that no local model produces (\code{nec\_e\_fine\_converse\_false}).
Fine's own theorem, in the form that speaks of correlations, is proved separately as an
equivalence: a table of four correlators comes from a local model exactly when each is between
$-1$ and $1$ and all eight CHSH inequalities hold (\code{nec\_e\_fine\_iff}, NecEFine.v). And one more thing about tallies, which the guarantee paragraph above already used: a free
tally is not thereby classical (the four rounds with S equal to four). The correlators (one,
one, one, minus one) are the PR box, which no classical play produces; the classical vertex this
section used, which fails the check like every plan, is (one, one, one, one).

[254] The calibration is a unit conversion
There's a function in the source that multiplies the counter by a chosen energy per mark,
and a theorem that a state with one mark, at the scale kT ln 2 per mark, carries kT ln 2 of
energy (\code{calibrated\_mu\_landauer\_energy}, proving the predicate \code{mu\_landauer\_calibration} for
every k and T). Read that for exactly what it is: a unit conversion, true by definition,
with the scale as a free input. Nothing in it picks the scale. The theorem would be just as
true with the scale set to a dollar a mark. I'd say that out loud, so the name
``Landauer'' in it does no work. What \emph{would} connect marks to joules is the premise in Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary},
\code{landauer\_heat}, and that one is an assumption with a name that nothing proves. And even
as a scale, $k_B T \ln 2$ a mark is the floor for one particular squeeze, a halving.
Corollary~\ref{cor:heat-floor}'s floor for a flip that brings $k$ states into a room already
holding $m$, starting
from the even spread on those $m+k$ states, is $k_B T \ln \frac{m+k}{m}$, and for one newcomer into a big room that's far below a
halving, roughly $k_B T / m$. Price every mark at $k_B T \ln 2$ and you can overstate the floor
of a single narrow flip by a factor that grows with $m$.

[255] The calibration is a unit conversion
Checked as \code{calibrated\_mu\_landauer\_energy} (PricingPhysicsAudit.v), which proves
\code{mu\_landauer\_calibration} (PricingPhysicsTarget.v) for all $k_B$ and $T$, and
\code{mu\_has\_no\_intrinsic\_joule\_value} (PricingPhysicsAudit.v). The physics is the premise of
Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}, restated here once in the form the next theorems test. In words first: a \emph{dissipation}
hands each instruction a whole number of heat units, each unit worth kT ln 2. The floor premise
says an instruction that takes at least one state where some yes-or-no
property is false to a state where it's true, never sends a true one back to false, and sends
two different states to one, dissipates at least one unit. That's a different statement from Landauer's principle as
Premise~\ref{prem:landauer} has it. That one depends on the spread over states,
$Q \ge k_B T \ln 2 \cdot \Delta H$, and gives nothing for a known state. This one gives every such
instruction a floor of one whole unit, whatever the spread. To get the second from the first
you'd need more, at the least a spread with a full bit of uncertainty removed at every such
step, and the known-state theorem and the narrow squeeze of Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}
show that isn't automatic. So I give it its own name, \emph{structural dissipation pricing}. The calibration says no instruction dissipates more units than the counter charges
it. One word about that word: ``dissipation'' here means heat handed to the surroundings, in the
sense Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary} took from Sagawa: a bill for where the forgotten bit goes.
It has nothing to do with waste, or with doing the job badly. Two premises, nothing proved about either,
and the only question is whether they can sit in the same room:

[256] The calibration is a unit conversion
The last clause of the floor is there because the first two don't make a step forget anything
on a machine with infinitely many states. INC $A$ sends a state with $A = 0$ to one with $A = 1$
and keeps every positive $A$ positive, so it raises ``$A \ge 1$'' one way, and it is
one-to-one. Any one-to-one map that misses some state does the same to the property ``is in my
image''. Such a step can be run backward, and Landauer's principle charges it nothing; a floor
read without the last clause would charge it a whole unit. On a finite machine the clause comes
for free. A step that sends one false state into the true ones and keeps every true state true
puts one more state into the true set than the set holds, so two of them land on the same
state. Checked as \code{nec\_f\_one\_way\_injective} and
\code{nec\_f\_finite\_one\_way\_merges} (NecFPhysics.v).

[257] The premises have to be able to hold together
Here's the one piece of this section that I think is good physics discipline, and it's a
consistency test. Suppose you want to claim the counter tracks dissipation. You need two
premises: that every step you've chosen to count dissipates at least one unit, and an upper
calibration that bounds dissipation by the charge. Can those two hold at once? There's a
theorem saying exactly when (\code{calibrated\_model\_exists\_iff\_positive\_cost}): a model
satisfying both exists precisely when every operation you've chosen to count (the statement
below calls those \emph{eligible}) costs at least one. I should say how cheap that is. The model the proof builds takes the dissipation to be
the cost itself, so the theorem shows the two premises don't contradict each other. It
doesn't show that the counter tracks any dissipation. Still, it does bite on a schedule: if you count a
zero-cost step, no such model can exist. One place the checked files show the premises holding is a
one-bit reset, where resetting costs one and idling costs
nothing (\code{bit\_model\_has\_satisfiable\_calibration}); the small machine's flag-raising steps, below, are another.

[258] The premises have to be able to hold together
Now point the test at the small machine of Section~\ref{sec:smallest}, counting every step
that collapses some yes-or-no property, and it fails. Read without the last clause of the
floor, it would fail at a step that forgets nothing: INC $A$ raises ``$A \ge 1$'' one way, is
one-to-one, and costs zero (\code{nec\_f\_small\_one\_way\_pair\_fails}, NecFPhysics.v). With
the clause in place the failure sits at a merge. Its free instructions merge states (the
last item of the list at the end of that chapter has the pair: two cores that one DEC sends to
the same core, for nothing), so DEC A 2, which collapses the property ``$A$ is zero or the line
number is 2'' by sending a state where it's false and a state where it's true to the same
state, costs zero there, and (1) below says no dissipation can charge it one and stay under its cost.
That's checked as \code{nec\_f\_small\_premise\_pair\_fails}, with the free merge itself as
\code{nec\_f\_small\_free\_collapse\_is\_merge} (NecFPhysics.v). The machine prices the
certifying chain and leaves its other merges free, so the premise pair holds for the steps
the toll is about (\code{nec\_f\_small\_premise\_pair\_on\_flag}) and fails for the whole instruction set. That's the honest shape.

[259] The premises have to be able to hold together
Checked as \code{calibrated\_model\_exists\_iff\_positive\_cost}, with the definition
\code{calibrated\_positive\_model}, and \code{bit\_model\_has\_satisfiable\_calibration}
(ObservationPolicy.v).

[260] The premises have to be able to hold together
Checked as \code{retained\_history\_step\_injective} (ObservationPolicy.v).

[261] The calorimeter protocol, as a specification
Here is how one \emph{would} measure this, and it's here because it makes the boundary exact. Take a two-state
register with energies zero and a gap, a population p in the excited state, and the simplest
possible rate equation for how p changes in one time step (a rate for climbing into the
excited state, a rate for falling out of it, and the length of the step). For the canonical reset
(population one half to zero, with the rates chosen so the equation closes), the heat
delivered to the bath is exactly half the gap (\code{canonical\_reset\_heat\_exact}). Choose the gap
to be twice kT ln 2 and that heat is exactly kT ln 2 (\code{selected\_gap\_gives\_landauer\_heat}).

[262] The calorimeter protocol, as a specification
Read that twice, because the temptation is to read it as a derivation of Landauer's number
and it's the opposite. The gap was \emph{chosen} to make the heat come out as Landauer's number.
The same populations and rates with half that gap give half the Landauer value, and there's
a theorem exhibiting identical population dynamics with different heat
(\code{master\_equation\_does\_not\_fix\_heat\_scale}). The rate equation fixes the \emph{shape}; the energy
scale is a selected Hamiltonian (the physicist's name for the rule that says what energy each
state has), and selecting it is not deriving it.

[263] The calorimeter protocol, as a specification
The canonical reset is a bookkeeping toy, and its rates show it. A register that trades heat
with a bath at temperature $T$ has rates in \emph{detailed balance}: the rate up over the rate
down is $e^{-\Delta/k_B T}$, which at the gap $2 k_B T \ln 2$ is $1/4$. The canonical rates are
0 and 1, and the factor $e^{-\Delta/k_B T}$ is never 0, so no gap and no temperature put them in
that ratio (\code{canonical\_rates\_break\_detailed\_balance}). With rates that are in it, a
relaxation at a fixed gap moves toward the equilibrium population $1/(1 + e^{\Delta/k_B T})$ and,
with steps short enough not to overshoot, never goes below it
(\code{detailed\_balance\_settles\_at\_gibbs}, \code{detailed\_balance\_never\_empties}). At the
gap $2 k_B T \ln 2$ that population is $1/5$ (\code{landauer\_gap\_settles\_at\_one\_fifth}). The
toy has no work term either; nothing in it pays for the squeeze. Part (4) of the theorem below
says what that costs: at the gap $k_B T \ln 2$ the toy books less heat than Landauer's bound
asks for the same squeeze from one half to zero, so the toy has no second law.

[264] The calorimeter protocol, as a specification
Checked as \code{canonical\_reset\_satisfies\_master\_equation}, \code{canonical\_reset\_heat\_exact},
\code{selected\_gap\_gives\_landauer\_heat}, \code{canonical\_reset\_heat\_below\_landauer\_at\_small\_gap} (given
$k_B > 0$ and $T > 0$) and
\code{master\_equation\_does\_not\_fix\_heat\_scale} (CalorimeterProtocol.v), with the
definitions in CalorimeterProtocolTarget.v.

[265] The calorimeter protocol, as a specification
Checked as \code{detailed\_balance\_settles\_at\_gibbs}, \code{detailed\_balance\_thermalizing\_step},
\code{driven\_first\_law}, \code{driven\_work\_second\_law}, \code{driven\_work\_near\_free\_energy} and
\code{driven\_reset\_work\_window} (CalorimeterProtocol.v), with the definitions in
CalorimeterProtocolTarget.v.

[266] What you're trusting, in one place
\begin{enumerate}
\item The statements. The checker confirms a proof proves its statement. It doesn't confirm the
statement says what my sentence about it says. That's why the book prints the important
ones in full, premises and all, and why Appendix~\ref{sec:coq-statements} prints the Coq text
of the load-bearing ones beside a line on whether my sentence says exactly that, more, or less. A
machine check supplements that reading.
\item Coq's kernel and its foundation, and the standard assumptions above.
\item Coq's bytecode machine, on the build's side. Coq can hand a computation inside a proof to a
virtual machine: a compiler from Coq terms to bytecode and an interpreter for that bytecode, written
in OCaml and C, which sit beside the kernel's ordinary reduction and are trusted when used. A proof
reaches it through the tactic \code{vm\_compute} or through a cast marked for it. The build uses it both through explicit calls to \code{vm\_compute}
and through arithmetic deciders (\code{lia}, \code{nia}, \code{lra},
\code{nra}, \code{psatz}) that check certificates on it; the vendored library's tactics for its
lambda-calculus files call \code{vm\_compute} too. When \texttt{coqc} builds those files, it
trusts the bytecode machine. Coq's independent checker, \texttt{coqchk}, keeps its bytecode
machine switched off unless it's told otherwise (its option \texttt{-bytecode-compiler} defaults to
no), the clean-reproduction procedure in COMPANION\_CHECKS.txt doesn't tell it otherwise, and with the machine
off the kernel checks each such step again by its ordinary reduction. So the bytecode machine is
trusted by the build, and the final pass doesn't lean on it. No proof uses \code{native\_compute},
the variant that compiles a computation to machine code through the OCaml compiler, so native
compilation adds nothing to this list.
\item The tools under Coq. Coq's own program, the compiler it was built with, the operating
system it ran on and the processor under all of them are trusted. This is the list Ken
Thompson's lecture from Section~\ref{sec:diagonal} was about. I didn't write Coq, or OCaml, or
any of the rest, so by his moral I can't fully trust any of them, and neither can you. What I
can do is keep the list short and print it, so you know exactly which tools you're being asked
to believe have no spare key cut into them.
\item The tested parts. The tests are evidence that the programs around the proofs do what's
claimed on the runs tried. They can't show the programs always do. Edsger Dijkstra had a sentence for
this, and the version in his 1976 book starts by admitting he'd already said it many times:
program testing ``can be quite effective for showing the presence of bugs, but is hopelessly
inadequate for showing their absence.'' (I'm quoting it as John Harrison, who was then proving
floating-point hardware correct at Intel, quotes it in a 2007 survey, from that
book.) That's why this item says \emph{tested}. It never says \emph{proved}.
\item The physics. Anywhere a theorem has Landauer's principle or a calorimeter in it, the
physical premise is named and assumed (Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}, Section~\ref{sec:landauer}).
Nothing here derives it.
\item Whoever sets the start and whoever reads the record. Every guarantee about the record is
about programs run from a clean start on a step that does what its definition says. The
operator who chooses the start, a reset, a device running the machine, and anyone reading a
report they didn't watch being made are outside it (Section~\ref{sec:scope} lists the whole
fence).
\end{enumerate}

[267] What you're trusting, in one place
One program on the install list is missing from this list on purpose. CSDP, a numerical solver for
semidefinite programs written by Brian Borchers, a mathematician at New Mexico Tech, is called by
one proof: \code{algebraically\_coherent\_tsirelson\_general}
(category/AlgebraicCoherence.v) ends with the tactic \code{psatz Q 4}, which hands the inequality
to CSDP. CSDP only proposes a certificate, a sum-of-squares identity with rational coefficients.
Coq then checks it again: a checker written in Coq, whose soundness is proved in Coq's standard
library, evaluates the certificate, and the kernel checks that evaluation. A wrong certificate
makes the tactic fail. It can't make a false statement pass. So CSDP is needed to build that
proof, and it's outside the trusted base.

[268] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{The universal floor (Section~\ref{sec:toll}).} Build a Thiele machine that satisfies the one-step toll
and give a run from uncertified to certified with total cost zero. \code{universal\_nfi\_any\_substrate}
falls. If you instead drop the toll, you've built a free-Thiele machine. That's
a different system, and it counts for nothing against the theorem.

[269] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{The toll from premises (Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}).} Exhibit a finite, duplicate-free list of every state, a
certificate no step revokes, and an \emph{injective} step that switches it on.
\code{permanent\_flip\_is\_not\_injective} falls, and the derivation of the toll with it. An infinite
state space, a revocable certificate, or an unpriced merge is not a counterexample; the file
already builds one of each. For the squeeze bound, a finite permanent machine with a cost
meeting the halving premise where one step switches k states on beside m certified ones and
two to the cost times m is less than m plus k; \code{permanent\_flips\_compression\_bound} falls.
For merge-or-revoke, a finite machine and an injective instruction that switches a reading on
without switching it off anywhere; \code{flip\_merges\_or\_revokes} falls. A move that merges and
also revokes counts against nothing. It's the three cards. For the eight-state
machine, a move of it that merges two states and costs nothing; \code{fin\_merging\_priced} falls.

[270] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{The bill in bits (Section~\ref{sec:a2-not-arbitrary}).} Exhibit a finite machine with a permanent certificate, a move that
switches k states on beside m certified ones (k at least one), and a spread of probability
over those m plus k states whose image under the move has entropy above log base two of m.
\code{permanent\_step\_entropy\_ceiling} falls. For the general form, a step and a spread on
a finite list of every state under which
two different states, each with some probability, land on the same state while the entropy
doesn't drop; \code{entropy\_drop\_pos\_of\_support\_merge} falls. A heat measurement on a real device
below what the bound asks doesn't touch either theorem. It refutes the premise
\code{landauer\_heat} for that device, which is Landauer's principle, and that's a fight with
physics. The counting stays where it was. And a certifying step from a state already known that gives
off no heat isn't a counterexample: \code{known\_state\_flip\_forces\_no\_heat} says the premise sets no positive
floor there.

[271] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{Learning is free (Section~\ref{sec:narrowing}).} Show that the four-state demon's measure step merges two states,
or that its observer's candidate list doesn't shrink. \code{observer\_narrowing\_can\_be\_free}
falls. A different price schedule that charges the measure step is allowed and isn't a
counterexample. The claim is ``not forced.'' It never says ``always free.''

[272] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{The counter (Section~\ref{sec:counter}; on the small machine, Section~\ref{sec:smallest}).}
Find a state of the small machine and an instruction such that the ledger after is anything
other than the ledger before plus that instruction's cost; the loud version is a ledger that
goes down. \code{mu\_conservation} falls. The uniqueness half, that only one bookkeeping starts
at zero and follows the schedule, is checked over any counter machine as
\code{nec\_f\_initiality} (Section~\ref{sec:counter}); a
reachable state where two such bookkeepings disagree makes it fall.

[273] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{Windows (Section~\ref{sec:windows}).} Exhibit a window and a price computed from the observed before and after
that meets the floor and never overcharges, on a machine where two steps share their before
and after pictures and only one certifies. \code{shadow\_cannot\_price\_exactly} falls. A window that
shows certification is not a counterexample; the theorem says exactly that such a window
prices exactly. For the three escapes, a verifier of one of the three kinds that's wrong about
the pair, under its own relation; the argument after Definition~\ref{def:u21-escape-relations}
falls.

[274] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{Entitlement (Section~\ref{sec:entitlement}).} Produce a strict narrowing of a candidate
list, with a distinguishing observation, that does not yield a strictly stronger membership
test; the card argument falls. Or exhibit a run with a tree no deeper than its paid steps,
covering the narrowing, whose counter rose by less than the index bits lost;
Theorem~\ref{thm:u08-count} falls.
On every certified Thiele-complete machine: a run from a clean start that ends with the record up, meets the
hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:ent-machine}, and has fewer record moves than the index bits it
narrowed, or has no earned chain in it; \code{ent\_representation} falls, and the weighted form
(\code{ent2\_weighted\_representation}) falls the same way. A narrowing with a big drop and a small
bill is not a counterexample unless a tree no deeper than the record moves covers it: the covering
is a hypothesis, and \code{ent2\_uncovered\_claim\_false} says why it has to stay one. On the host
machine, a run from a clean start that raises the record only after more than sixteen CHECKs; \code{ent2\_questions\_cap}
falls. On the small machine, a program of INC and DEC that runs fewer than $m$ moves and
whose final line number and $B$
determine whether $A \ge m$ at every start; \code{ent2\_free\_cannot\_decide\_fast} falls.

[275] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{The latch (Section~\ref{sec:latch}).} Exhibit an extension of a base whose record is driven by the computation
and never switches off, and which is not a latch on any event. \code{record\_axis\_is\_latch\_holds}
falls. A record that switches off, or one driven by a hidden clock, is the file's own pair of
counterexamples. The file built those already.

[276] The abstract claims (Parts II and III)
\emph{The diagonal (Section~\ref{sec:diagonal}).} Two routes. A decider for some substrate
with a shortcut predicate whose flip the substrate can represent and which answers
correctly on every program; the generic proof fails at a named step. For the nat family, a d
that correctly decides the predicate of the substrate built from d. L has its recursion theorem
by proof, so it gets a target of its own: a map on L's terms, computable by an L term, with no
term that behaves like its own image; \code{L\_recursion\_theorem} falls.
On the host machine: a host program that decides an extensional property of host programs that some
program has and some lacks, or a map of host programs that a host program carries out, with no host
program that computes the same function as its image; \code{sm\_host\_rice} or \code{sm\_kleene}
falls. On the small machine: a decider for a property of what two-counter programs do from the clean
start that some program has and some lacks; \code{sm\_guest\_rice} falls. A decider for such a
property over every input of two-counter programs; \code{tc\_rice} falls. A two-counter program that
multiplies every plain input by the number of Theorem~\ref{thm:no-plain-recursion}; \code{tc2\_no\_mult}
falls. A map of host programs that a host program carries out, with no host
program that stops when its image stops and agrees with it on register 0, the trap latch, the ledger,
the flag, the number of facts and whether the channel is empty, is a counterexample to
\code{sm2\_kleene\_obs}. A map whose fixed point disagrees with its image on some other register is not one:
some computable map has no fixed point that agrees with its image on every register (\code{sm2\_no\_exact}).

[277] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{The small machine (Section~\ref{sec:smallest}).} Four targets. From a clean start, a
run that raises the flag without an earlier passing CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY of the same
claim, with the counter's version unchanged between the CHECK and the COMMIT;
\code{earned\_certification\_provenance} falls, and \code{earned\_commitment\_provenance} for
a COMMIT alone. From a clean start, a trace that leaves a fact in the table nobody checked;
\code{no\_forging} falls. From a clean start, a run that ends certified for a total charge
under three; \code{certified\_run\_min\_cost} falls, and a run at exactly three is not a
counterexample: CHECK ``A is 0'', COMMIT, CERTIFY from a start where A is 0 is one, and the
same program from any other start is refused forever (\code{earned\_run\_check\_can\_fail},
\code{earned\_run\_refused\_forever}). A check that can't fail certifying for three isn't a
counterexample either; the step doesn't police trivial checks, and the book says so. A two-counter machine and a
program whose run on the small machine disagrees with it step for step, or halts when it
doesn't; \code{simulation\_step} or \code{halting\_correspondence} falls.

[278] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{Certified Thiele-complete (Section~\ref{sec:smallest}).} A machine that charges at least one for every
move and still meets the four clauses; \code{complete\_has\_free\_move} falls, and with it \code{clock\_not\_thiele\_complete}, whose proof uses it. A
certified Thiele-complete machine and a function of its window that gives its record at the end of every
run from every loaded state; \code{complete\_no\_record\_oracle} falls. A clause none of the
excluded machines needs: drop it and show the paid latch or the silent machine still fails, and
\code{paid\_latch\_not\_thiele\_complete} or \code{silent\_not\_thiele\_complete} has been proved
by the wrong clause, which is worth a letter.

[279] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{The universal program (Section~\ref{sec:universal-host}).} A program of the small machine and
a start on which $U$ halts and the guest doesn't, or the other way round;
\code{universal\_halting} falls. One on which the flags disagree, or the ledgers differ at a
matching point; \code{universal\_flag\_iff} or \code{universal\_ledger\_exact} falls. A run of $U$
whose raised flag doesn't stand on a passing CHECK, then COMMIT, of one slot, then CERTIFY;
\code{universal\_earned} falls.

[280] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{Every computably presented machine (Section~\ref{sec:presented-universal}).} A computably
presented Thiele machine and a start on which $U_P$'s flag goes up while the machine's reading
never says yes, or the other way round; \code{presented\_universal\_flag\_iff} falls. With the
reading starting at no, a step the machine reaches at which every point of
$U_P$'s run that matches it has a ledger more than two above the
machine's; \code{presented\_universal\_surcharge\_le\_two} falls. A program on the host machine,
from a clean start, that matches exactly a guest certified with its whole ledger below three;
\code{presented\_universal\_no\_exact\_below\_three} falls. A machine whose reading no program
decides isn't a counterexample. It's outside the class, and the book says so.

[281] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{The classical CHSH bound (Section~\ref{sec:chsh}).} Exhibit four fixed bits and a tally consistent with that
one plan, every pair sampled, scoring above two. \code{chsh\_violation\_rules\_out\_locally\_factorizable\_coupling}
falls, and so does known mathematics. A randomised plan landing past two by luck doesn't
count; the theorem is about one fixed plan, and Section~\ref{sec:chsh}'s coin-flip example shows the luck.

[282] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{The integer check (Section~\ref{sec:chsh}).} The game check has two walls, and here's how to knock each one
over. A correlator that passes the three contraction inequalities whose
five-by-five is not PSD (forward), or a PSD five-by-five of that shape whose correlators
aren't contractive (reverse); \code{npa\_psd\_iff\_column\_contractive} falls. A correlator whose
pinned five-by-five is PSD (contractive, by the equivalence) with S squared above eight;
\code{npa\_psd\_implies\_tsirelson\_bound} falls. The PR box and the classical mixture (1, 0, 1, 0) are
\emph{not} counterexamples to any of these. Both fail contraction by plain arithmetic (the table
in Appendix~\ref{sub:chsh-five}), which is exactly what the theorems need them to
do.
On the machine: a raised flag from a clean start with no passing CHECK of the packed-counts property
before it, or whose counts give $S^2 > 8$; \code{small\_chsh\_flag\_implies\_tsirelson} falls. A
tally that passes the seven facts while its pinned matrix isn't PSD, or one with every pair sampled
and a PSD pinned matrix that fails them; \code{small\_chsh\_check\_iff} falls. The all-ones tally and
the PR box being refused are not counterexamples; those refusals are the point.

[283] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{The nine-by-nine (Section~\ref{sec:chsh}).} A point and five extra numbers where the
contraction-like condition holds and the nine-by-nine isn't PSD, or the other way round;
\code{q1ab\_psd\_iff\_column\_contractive} falls. A point where the condition holds with all five
extras at zero and S is above two; \code{q1ab\_check\_at\_gzero\_implies\_classical\_bound} falls.

[284] The machine claims (Part IV)
\emph{The elliptope completion (Section~\ref{sec:chsh}).} This one's a shape, so you're trying
to push a point in or out of it. Three targets, one per theorem, plus the decider beside it. Signs $a_0, a_1, b_0, b_1$,
each squaring to one, and a vector on which the completed matrix of that plan goes negative;
\code{deterministic\_strategy\_elliptope} falls. A correlator with some positive semidefinite
completion and S squared above eight; \code{elliptope\_tsirelson}
falls. Real numbers x and y that
make the PR box's completed matrix positive semidefinite; \code{pr\_box\_not\_elliptope} falls. And
for the decider that sits beside it: inputs on which it says yes while the correlators have
no PSD completion at all (S squared above eight is one way to show that);
\code{elliptope\_check\_full\_sound} falls. The all-ones plan and (1, 0, 1, 0) aren't counterexamples here either, from the other side: the
completion admits them (\code{turing\_point\_elliptope} for the second), which is what the theorems
require.

A decoder needs constant fibres & proved (any types) & Section~\ref{sec:windows}, Theorem~\ref{thm:fibre} \\
No exact window price across a collision & proved (premise: a collision), by unfolding & Section~\ref{sec:windows}, Observation~\ref{thm:shadow-price} \\
\end{longtable}

The 1+AB matrix ties its four-body cells, the constraint $\langle \{A_0, A_1\} \otimes \{B_0, B_1\} \rangle = 0$; the level-1 pinnings don't imply it, and a classical strategy breaks it & proved (\code{q1ab\_tie\_is\_a\_constraint}, QuantumPartitionPSD\_\allowbreak{}1AB.v) & Section~\ref{sec:chsh}, Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation} \\
A tally above two isn't one plan replayed & proved & Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation}, Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-tally} \\
A tally above two proves nonlocality; a pass proves the data quantum & not claimed & Section~\ref{sec:chsh} \\
A finite local model meets the bound & proved; Fine's theorem in correlation form is proved as an iff (\code{nec\_e\_fine\_iff}), and the converse of \code{fine\_theorem} as stated is false (\code{nec\_e\_fine\_converse\_false}, NecEFine.v) & Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation}, Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-fine} \\
The elliptope gate is sound; the PR box fails it & proved & Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation}, Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-gate-sound} \\
The arc-sine boundary: a table has a PSD completion, and so is quantum, exactly when its arcsines pass the four inequalities {\footnotesize(if and only if)} & proved & Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation} \\
Every PSD completion comes from a quantum strategy, and the completion set is exactly the set of quantum correlator tables in finite dimension, real or complex & proved & Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation} \\
A two-qubit strategy reaches $2\sqrt{2}$; every strategy in finite dimension, with real or complex amplitudes, has $S^2 \le 8$ and a PSD level-1 moment matrix; every deterministic plan is such a strategy & proved & Appendix~\ref{sec:chsh-derivation} \\
The integer route to the bound is new mathematics & not claimed; the route is NPA's & Section~\ref{sec:chsh} \\
The integer check passes exactly when every pair was sampled and the pinned matrix is PSD & proved & Section~\ref{sec:chsh}, Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-exact} \\
Honest play at Tsirelson's point fails the exact check about 85 percent of the time & simulated, with a first-order limit of $(2 + \sqrt 2)/4$; no theorem & Section~\ref{sec:chsh} \\
The check with a tolerance $\varepsilon$ is the exact check at $\varepsilon = 0$; a pass means operator norm at most $1 + \varepsilon$ and $S^2 \le 8(1 + \varepsilon)^2$ & proved (\code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_zero}, \code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_norm}, \code{nec\_e\_tol\_check\_bound}, NecEChsh.v); earned CHECK integration: \code{small\_chsh\_tol\_flag\_bound} (SmallChshTolerance.v) & Section~\ref{sec:chsh} \\
A flag raised on the host machine from a clean start stands on a passing CHECK of the packed counts, a COMMIT and a CERTIFY; the chain run from a fresh start certifies for 3 exactly when the check passes & proved & Section~\ref{sec:chsh}, Theorem~\ref{thm:chsh-earned} \\
Five tallies: 12/5 and 14/5 certify; 16/5, the PR box and the all-ones plan are refused forever & proved & Section~\ref{sec:chsh}, Proposition~\ref{prop:chsh-demos} \\
The tally was played, or was honest, or puts a bound on nature & not claimed; it is placed in a counter for free & Section~\ref{sec:chsh} \\
The calibration is a unit conversion & definition; proved & Section~\ref{sec:landauer}, Definition~\ref{def:u24-scale}, Theorem~\ref{thm:u24-calibration} \\
Structural dissipation pricing (a whole-unit floor on collapsing instructions, separate from Landauer's principle); dissipation at most cost & assumed (premise) & Section~\ref{sec:landauer}, Premise~\ref{prem:u24-landauer-pair} \\
The premises fit when every counted step costs at least one, and fit a one-bit reset & proved & Section~\ref{sec:landauer}, Theorem~\ref{thm:u24-consistency} \\
A collapse needs the merge clause: INC $A$ raises ``$A \ge 1$'' one way, one-to-one and free; on a finite machine a one-way step always merges & proved (\code{nec\_f\_one\_way\_injective}, \code{nec\_f\_small\_one\_way\_pair\_fails}, \code{nec\_f\_finite\_one\_way\_merges}, NecFPhysics.v) & Section~\ref{sec:landauer}, Premise~\ref{prem:u24-landauer-pair} \\
The small machine's whole instruction set fails the premise pair & proved (\code{nec\_f\_small\_premise\_pair\_fails}); its free steps merge (\code{nec\_f\_small\_free\_collapse\_is\_merge}, NecFPhysics.v) & Section~\ref{sec:landauer} \\
Keeping history makes every step injective & proved & Section~\ref{sec:landauer}, Theorem~\ref{thm:u24-history} \\
The heat is fixed by the chosen gap & definition; proved & Section~\ref{sec:landauer}, Definition~\ref{def:u24-calorimeter}, Theorem~\ref{thm:u24-calorimeter} \\
The canonical reset's rates are in detailed balance at no gap; with rates that are, a fixed-gap relaxation settles at $1/(1 + e^{\Delta/k_B T})$, which is $1/5$ at the gap $2 k_B T \ln 2$ & proved (\code{canonical\_rates\_break\_detailed\_balance}, \code{detailed\_balance\_never\_empties}, \code{landauer\_gap\_settles\_at\_one\_fifth}, CalorimeterProtocol.v) & Section~\ref{sec:landauer} \\
A driven reset with rates in detailed balance pays between $kT \ln 2 - kT e^{-D/kT}$ and $kT \ln 2 + D/(2N)$ in work & definition; proved & Section~\ref{sec:landauer}, Definition~\ref{def:u24-driven}, Theorem~\ref{thm:u24-driven} \\
The counter is physical dissipation, in joules & not claimed & Section~\ref{sec:landauer} \\
Permanence predicts more than Landauer & not claimed & Section~\ref{sec:landauer} \\
The theorems prove physical reality, or Coq's consistency & not claimed & Section~\ref{sec:methodology} \\
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

[294] Scope
\emph{Trace initiality, and what it doesn't settle.} The universal property of the trace fold is
about evaluating lists of instructions: pick where a target starts, and there's exactly one
way to fold a list into it (Section~\ref{sec:counter}, checked as
\code{nec\_f\_fold\_unique}). The counter's uniqueness
assumes a schedule. Rise-only pricing assumes an event, and a reference count that says what
charging too much means (Section~\ref{sec:windows}). Those results make the construction precise. None of them
shows that the schedule I chose, or the event I chose, is physically necessary.

[295] Scope
\emph{The record axis.} The proved structural result is: a permanent record driven by a base
computation is a latch on an event; on a finite machine its write merges states; and the named
windows don't recover it. Certification is one instance. No theorem
selects it as the unique event every system must record (Section~\ref{sec:latch}, Section~\ref{sec:pointer}). A finite monotone
multi-valued record decomposes into threshold latches. One Boolean latch can't hold it.
Revocation, including revocation priced from the other side as in Casper, lies outside the
permanent-record axis; a price on revocation doesn't transfer to a price on the write (Section~\ref{sec:models}).
Probabilistic records don't inherit the uniqueness result either. Two honest random records
can have the same branches and the same charges and still give those branches different
weights, one choosing evenly and one two to one, so the schedule doesn't fix the
probabilities (\code{schedule\_determines\_probabilities\_refuted}). And no single deterministic
latch explains a record that can branch both ways from the same observation
(\code{deterministic\_latch\_handles\_branching\_refuted}).
Weak base equivalence is defined and is an equivalence; no two of the three bases of
Definition~\ref{def:u11-concrete-bases}, and none of them and the small machine, are related by it in
the checked work, and the latch form holds on each separately, so it doesn't tell
them apart (Section~\ref{sec:latch}).
A record driven by the computation that stays up is a latch, and on a finite machine its write merges states;
which event deserves the latch isn't proved; records that can come back down lie outside all
of this (they're limit computability's, and Section~\ref{sec:latch} points at Ershov's ladder),
and random ones lose the uniqueness.

[296] Scope
\emph{The comparison machines.} The small machines I built to compare against are fragments too.
The random-access memory result covers an addressed list memory with load, increment and
decrement on cells (\code{concrete\_ram\_write\_reads\_back} and its neighbours), and it's about that
machine. The register machine Section~\ref{sec:latch} puts the latch on (\code{record\_axis\_is\_latch\_on\_ram\_holds}) is
a different small machine, the one with indirect addressing, and that theorem is about it. The reversible-machine
result covers arithmetic update cores: add and subtract, each undone by the other, with or
without wrapping at a modulus (\code{janus\_like\_unbounded\_inverse}, \code{janus\_like\_bounded\_inverse}).
None is a theorem about real memories or full reversible languages. The cost-semantics
results are local correspondences, short of full cost-framework embeddings: the trace's cost is a
writer cost and a grade, and the toll is the potential method with one potential (Section~\ref{sec:counter}).
Nobody's whole type system gets embedded either.

[297] Scope
\emph{Outward consequences.} I looked for results about real systems that would need the record
axis. Five narrow ones are proved, each as a two-state countermodel: a certificate-transparency
client's local tree head can't decide whether the world's log is consistent
(\code{ct\_local\_view\_insufficient}); a quote check that matches only the composite digest accepts a
mismatched selection (\code{tpm\_selection\_binding\_is\_necessary}); a local suffix can't decide a
trusted anchor (\code{weak\_subjective\_suffix\_insufficient}); an acknowledgement without a durable
commit record can be lost (\code{wal\_ack\_requires\_durability}); and a current local log can't
decide what happened before it (\code{audit\_local\_snapshot\_insufficient}). Each reduces to a known
indistinguishability or durability argument. Five stronger candidates would need protocol or
hardware semantics I haven't formalised, and I've proved nothing about them. So none of this
yields a new outside result that both needs the record axis and is ready to use.

[298] Conclusion
And the two computers from the first pages, the sorter and the copier, holding the same bits.
Walk the warehouse again. On an ordinary machine there's still no lamp for what the sorter did,
and nothing in this book changes that. On the small machine there is one: a flag and a counter
you can read off the state, and a theorem that, from a clean start, the flag can't be up unless
some claim went through a passing check, a commit about the same edition and a certificate, in that order, where a claim can be any
property, in a language with an exact
equality test, whose checker is proved to mean what it says, ``this counter, read as a list, is
sorted'' among them (\code{sorted\_run\_certifies\_iff} and \code{sorted\_run\_refused\_forever}, EarnedGeneric.v). That's the one lamp I set
out to light. It's a lamp for whoever can read this machine's state. A ``tests passed'' sheet
fits it only when the test is a checker proved to mean what it says; the sticker and the very
sure AI from the first pages ask something else, whether to believe a report you didn't watch
being made, and nothing in this book answers that (Section~\ref{sec:scope}). Whether every
machine ought to have that lamp is the argument. No proof here settles it.

[299] Words this book builds
The \emph{counter}, which I also call the \emph{ledger}: in the model, a number carried in the state
that starts at zero and that every step raises by exactly that step's charge (Section~\ref{sec:counter}). My
machine has exactly one, the ledger $\mu$ (Section~\ref{sec:smallest}). Two other things get called
counters, and each chapter says which: Minsky's two bins on the small machine, and the eight counts that
tally the game's rounds (Section~\ref{sec:chsh}). Neither is the ledger. A \emph{price list} says
what each move costs; the model takes it as given, and my machine's table of what each instruction
costs is in Section~\ref{sec:smallest}. A \emph{receipt} is a total that lives in the
state, only climbs, equals the sum of the charges on every path including the failures, and is
the only such total for its price list (Section~\ref{sec:counter} proves it for any system with a counter
and a price list, \code{nec\_f\_conservation}, \code{nec\_f\_counter\_monotone} and
\code{nec\_f\_initiality}; on my machine the sum is a checked theorem too, Section~\ref{sec:smallest}). In
the model there is just a \emph{certification reading}, yes or no (Section~\ref{sec:toll}); on
the small machine it's the \emph{flag}, set by a CERTIFY that passes and never cleared.

[300] Words this book builds
These belong to the small machine only (Section~\ref{sec:smallest}). A \emph{two-counter machine} is Minsky's: two
counters holding whole numbers with no upper limit, and two instructions: add one, and
subtract one and jump if the counter is above zero, or else go on to the next line; already enough that nobody can decide in general whether
one halts. The small machine is a two-counter machine plus HALT and three instructions for the
record, six in all: INC, DEC, HALT, CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY. A counter's \emph{version} is its
edition number: it starts at 0 and goes up by one every time the counter is written, so an
edition never comes back. A \emph{fact} is a property, a counter and a version together, such as
``$A$ is even, as of edition 3''; the properties come from a fixed language of three, \emph{is zero},
\emph{is even} and \emph{is at least $n$}. The same theorems also hold for any property language
with an exact
equality test whose checker is proved to mean what it says, and one such language reads a counter as a list
and asks whether it's sorted (\code{sorted\_run\_certifies\_iff} and \code{sorted\_run\_refused\_forever}, EarnedGeneric.v). The \emph{fact table} holds up to sixteen facts, and the only way
in is a CHECK that passed; nothing ever leaves it. The small machine's \emph{channel} holds one
committed fact or nothing, and COMMIT puts a fact there only if the table has it about the
counter's current edition. With the flag up from a clean start it names a commitment earned
that way, and a later COMMIT
replaces it, as a two-commit run shows (\code{certified\_channel\_earned} and \code{channel\_names\_last\_commit}, EarnedCore.v). The \emph{trap} is a bit that goes up when an instruction refuses, and
then nothing in the core moves again. A commitment is \emph{earned} when a passing check put its
fact in the table first, about the same edition, with no write to that counter in between; on
the small machine every flag raised from a clean start is earned, and that's a theorem.
CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY cost one each and everything else costs nothing,
so from a clean start a run ending with the flag up adds at least three to the ledger, and a run whose check could have failed adds exactly three: CHECK
\emph{is zero} on $A$, COMMIT, CERTIFY, from a start where $A$ is 0. The step doesn't police
trivial checks; \emph{is at least 0} passes every value and certifies for three too. The
\emph{host machine} is the small machine with a counter, called a \emph{register}, for every
whole number, and one property, $\mathsf{Slot}$, true of a number that packs a property of the
small machine together with a value it holds of. A \emph{slot} is a register of the host used for
nothing but holding one checked fact about the guest, and its \emph{mirror} is a register beside
it, safe to read, that says which claim the slot holds while its fact is live. $U$ is the fixed
program of 3728 host instructions that runs every small-machine program; it reads guest
instructions out of one register, keeps the guest's counters in two others, and puts every
checked fact in a slot, because on this machine reading a counter writes it. The universal
program's \emph{guest} is a program of the small machine, held as one number in a register of the
host and run one instruction at a time by $U$'s fetch and decode. $U_P$ is $U$ with one more
handler, for PAY, an instruction that costs one and does nothing else; its guests are programs
compiled from computably presented Thiele machines (Section~\ref{sec:presented-universal}).

[301] The records that carry a rule
\begin{definition}[Thiele machine]\label{def:app-certsys}
The record \code{CertificationSystem} (UniversalCertificationCost.v) has five data fields, a state
type, a move type, a step function, a cost per move in $\mathbb{N}$, and a yes/no reading,
and one proof field, \code{cs\_cert\_costs}: a move that turns the reading from no to yes costs at
least one. This proof field is the toll of Section~\ref{sec:toll}, and in this record's
numbering it is called A2. Every result stated ``for any Thiele machine'' assumes it,
because the record cannot be built without it.
\end{definition}

[302] The records that carry a rule

\begin{definition}[Quantitative Thiele machine]\label{def:app-qcs}
The record \code{QuantitativeCertificationSystem} (QuantitativeNoFI.v) extends a certification
system with a \emph{witness}, a function $W$ from states to $\mathbb{N}$, and a threshold
$N \in \mathbb{N}$, and adds two proof fields:
(A3, \code{qcs\_cost\_bounds\_witness}) $W(s) + \mathrm{cost}(i) \ge W(\mathrm{step}(s, i))$, the
witness grows by at most what the move cost; and
(A5, \code{qcs\_cert\_threshold\_witness}) a state that reads yes has $W(s) \ge N$.
An optional extension, \code{QuantitativeCertificationSystem\_full}, adds
(A4, \code{qcsf\_witness\_nondecreasing}) $W(s) \le W(\mathrm{step}(s, i))$.
\end{definition}

[303] The records that carry a rule
Checked as \code{universal\_nfi\_quantitative} (QuantitativeNoFI.v). A3 and A4 bound opposite
directions, and neither implies the other: witness 1, cost 0, witness 0 satisfies A3 and
breaks A4, and witness 0, cost 0, witness 1 does the reverse. The record is a general shape.
The small machine's own floor of three is proved directly (Observation~\ref{thm:u34-min-price})
and never goes through it.

[304] The records that carry a rule

\midrule
\endhead

\endlastfoot
A2 & the toll: field \code{cs\_cert\_costs} of \code{CertificationSystem} \\
A3 & the witness grows by at most the cost: field \code{qcs\_cost\_bounds\_witness} \\
A4 & the witness never drops: field \code{qcsf\_witness\_nondecreasing} of the full record \\

\end{longtable}

[305] The premises the derivations take
\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.233\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.221\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.221\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.221\linewidth}@{}}

\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot

\end{longtable}
